Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
On February 1, 2022, GlaxoSmithKline, Inc. submitted NDA 216951 to the FDA for approval of
daprodustat for the treatment of anemia associated with chronic kidney disease (CKD) in adults
on dialysis and not on dialysis.
If approved, daprodustat would be the first-in-class inhibitor of hypoxia inducible factor-prolyl
hydroxylase (HIF-PH), which improves anemia of CKD by inducing endogenous production of
erythropoietin, rather than supplementing it exogenously. Daprodustat’s oral administration route
differs from the parenteral administration route of erythropoietin stimulating agents (ESAs), the
current standard of care (together with iron supplementation) for treating anemia associated with
CKD.
Hematologic response has been used to establish efficacy for traditional approval for drugs
intended to treat anemia of CKD. In head-to-head, randomized, controlled trials, daprodustat
increased hemoglobin (Hb) to a similar extent as approved ESAs in the non-dialysis dependent
(NDD) population and in the dialysis dependent (DD) population with similar continued need for
red blood cell (RBC) transfusions or rescue therapy. Daprodustat was also efficacious in raising
Hb in the NDD population in a randomized, placebo-controlled trial. These multiple adequate
and well-controlled trials establish substantial evidence of effectiveness of daprodustat for the
treatment of anemia in patients with CKD, for both the NDD and DD populations. The trials did
not establish any other benefits on how patients feel, function, or survive.
Safety findings for the two populations diverged. For the DD population, for the co-primary
safety analysis, daprodustat demonstrated non-inferiority to the ESAs for adjudicated major
adverse cardiovascular events (MACE, defined as a composite of all-cause mortality, nonfatal
myocardial infarction, and nonfatal stroke). In addition, daprodustat did not have unfavorable
effects on other cardiovascular events (e.g., myocardial infarction (MI), stroke,
thromboembolism, vascular access thrombosis) compared to darbepoetin alfa or epoetin alfa in
the DD population, with the exception of hospitalization for heart failure (HHF). However, this
risk was particularly confined to those patients with a history of heart failure. Daprodustat also
appears to increase the risk of bleeding gastric and esophageal erosions. The clinical review
team, statistical team, and division director are aligned with the position that the benefits
outweigh the risks in the DD population as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework below, and
the office director, who is the signatory authority, concurs. While the advantage of an oral route
is less clear for the vast majority of patients undergoing hemodialysis in the U.S. who typically
receive an ESA during the dialysis session, it will provide another treatment option and will be
preferred by some patients over parenteral administration of ESAs.
For detailed information supporting the basis for this action, refer to the detailed sections
included in this Interdisciplinary Assessment document.
In the NDD population, for the co-primary safety endpoint of MACE, daprodustat demonstrated
non-inferiority to ESAs. However, in contrast to the DD population, daprodustat showed several
other important risks in addition to the risks of heart failure and bleeding gastric erosions. Our
3
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

findings, based on adjudicated cardiovascular endpoints, show elevated HRs for stroke,
thromboembolic disease, vascular access thrombosis, and to a lesser extent MI, relative to the
ESA comparator, which itself already carries these risks. The estimated harms were further
elevated in the U.S. subgroup of patients, which is noteworthy because daprodustat will be used
in the U.S. if approved. Lastly, we also identified a possible increased risk of acute kidney injury
with daprodustat in the NDD population. The oral route also has potential downsides in this
population, which is not as closely followed as the DD population, such as risks of inadequate
Hb monitoring, which might lead to worse outcomes (e.g., if Hb is increased too much or too
quickly).
We could not identify a risk mitigation strategy or postmarketing approach that will allow for the
safe administration of daprodustat at the proposed dosing regimen in the NDD population to
ensure the benefits of daprodustat outweigh its risks. The clinical team, statistical team, and
division director conclude that the overall benefit/risk is unfavorable in the non-dialysis
population as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework below, and the office director, who is the
signatory authority, concurs. For detailed information supporting the basis for this action, refer to
the detailed sections included in this Interdisciplinary Assessment document.

compared to ESA for other known risks of ESAs, including
malignancy, seizure, hypertension, infection, or hypersensitivity
reactions.

Conclusions and Reasons
Other notable risks included HHF (particularly in patients with
a history of heart failure), serious AKI, and bleeding
gastric/esophageal erosions.
In the DD population: ASCEND-D:
Daprodustat demonstrated non-inferiority for time to first
adjudicated MACE comparing daprodustat to epoetin alfa
(hemodialysis) and darbeopoetin alfa (peritoneal dialysis).
Unlike the results for the NDD population, there was not a
consistent pattern of increased risk estimates in the CV
endpoints (CV mortality, MI, stroke, thromboembolic disease,
and vascular access thrombosis) for daprodustat vs. ESA.
However, similar to the results for the NDD population, there
were other notable risks including HHF (particularly in
patients with a history of heart failure) and bleeding
gastric/esophageal erosions.
Daprodustat and the ESA comparators had comparable risks
of worsening hypertension.
Whether daprodustat may increase the risk of malignancy
like ESAs is uncertain. While such a risk was not seen in the
completed trials, their duration and the number of malignancy
events are insufficient to definitively exclude a risk.
Given the more reassuring findings when daprodustat is
compared to ESA in the DD population, there is a favorable
benefit/risk assessment in this population, with the oral route
of administration offering another treatment option that some
may prefer over the parenteral ESAs.
Consistent with the approach used with other products for
treatment of anemia in CKD (ESAs), the risks of daprodustat
can be sufficiently mitigated in the DD population with
labeling:
 Because the trials demonstrated non-inferiority on MACE
with respect to the ESA comparators, a similar boxed
warning will be added for the increased risk of death,

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
Unmet Need
CKD is a highly prevalent condition in the United States. Anemia associated with CKD is
common but can be serious if severe and left untreated. Treatment options for the anemia of
CKD include iron (oral or injection), ESAs, and RBC transfusions. Risks with RBC transfusions
include alloreactivity (a risk factor for renal allograft rejection in CKD patients who
subsequently undergo renal transplantation), volume overload, iron overload, hyperkalemia,
infection, and hypersensitivity reactions. ESAs, as current standard of care, are effective at
increasing and maintaining hemoglobin levels but are associated with significant cardiovascular
and thrombotic adverse reactions, especially when higher hemoglobin values are targeted. There
is an unmet need for safer products and orally available therapies that allow patients to avoid the
need for ESA injections and RBC transfusions.
Benefits
Daprodustat’s evidence of effectiveness for the treatment of anemia due to CKD in adults is
based primarily on ASCEND-ND and ASCEND-D, which are event-driven, multicenter,
multinational, open-label, active-controlled, randomized, parallel-group trials. ASCEND-ND
compared daprodustat to darbepoetin alfa (SC) in the NDD population. ASCEND-D compared
daprodustat to darbepoetin alfa (SC) for those on peritoneal dialysis and to epoetin alfa (IV) for
those on hemodialysis.
Daprodustat demonstrated non-inferiority to the approved ESA comparators in raising and
maintaining hemoglobin levels up to a treatment period of 52 weeks in subjects with CKD and
anemia in both the NDD and DD populations. Rates of RBC transfusions were similar in the
treatment arms. We expect the similarity in Hb response and similarity in the need for rescue
therapy between daprodustat and the ESA control arms to translate into similar reductions in the
need for RBC transfusions, an accepted clinical benefit for treatments for anemia of CKD,
because of avoiding the procedure and its attendant risks.
Daprodustat is orally administered compared to the parenteral administration route of ESAs. The
benefit of an oral agent is most apparent for the NDD population, which does not frequent health
care facilities as often as the DD population. The benefit of the oral route is less clear for most
DD patients receiving in-center dialysis where ESAs are typically given as part of the dialysis
session. The oral route may reduce the need to travel for parenteral ESA (e.g., for patients on
home dialysis), although this benefit is also less clear when compared to self-administration of
subcutaneous ESAs. Regardless, the oral route will provide another treatment option and will be
preferred by some patients.
The trials established no other benefits of daprodustat in the NDD-CKD or DD-CKD
populations. Evidence of improved quality of life was not demonstrated convincingly.
Risks
In both ASCEND-D and ASCEND-ND trials, daprodustat demonstrated non-inferiority with
respect to the ESAs on the time to first occurrence of MACE (defined as a composite of all-cause
mortality, nonfatal myocardial infarction, and nonfatal stroke).
12
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

In the NDD population, there were treatment differences not favoring daprodustat for
adjudicated cardiovascular endpoints, including stroke, thromboembolism, including vascular
access thrombosis, and to a lesser extent, MI. These CV risks (with the exception of stroke),
appear higher in the U.S. subgroup of patients compared to the non-U.S. subgroup. There
remains uncertainty as to whether there is a true increase in harm associated with daprodustat use
relative to darbepoetin on these CV outcomes. However, the consistent pattern of unfavorable
treatment differences among different risks raise the level of concern. Because the ESAs already
carry these risks, an apparent further increase in these risks beyond that seen with the ESAs is
concerning and not supportive of approval.
In ASCEND-ND, we also noted a treatment difference not favoring daprodustat in adjudicated
HHF (particularly in those with a history of heart failure), investigator-reported serious
gastrointestinal erosions (mostly related to gastrointestinal bleeding, and over half required
transfusion), and a risk for investigator-reported serious acute kidney injury.
In the DD population, the safety signals seen with the NDD population were not observed in the
DD population except for the risks of HHF (again, particularly in those with a history of HF) and
serious gastric erosions (which appeared less notable than that seen in the NDD population).
(b) (4)

Benefit/Risk in the DD Population
The increased risk for HHF was also observed in the DD population, especially in the subgroup
with a history of heart failure. Acknowledging the precision and post-hoc nature of this subgroup
13
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

analysis, there is a plausible mechanism for the subgroup interaction and an appreciable effect
modification observed in both the ASCEND-ND and ASCEND-D studies. For these reasons, we
concluded that the HHF risk could be mitigated in the DD population by including a warning for
patients with a history of heart failure. In the DD population in contrast to the NDD population,
there was not a consistent pattern of increased risk estimates in the other CV endpoints (CV
mortality, MI, stroke, thromboembolic disease, and vascular access thrombosis). The risk of
serious erosions will be included as a warning so that healthcare providers consider it when
deciding whether to prescribe daprodustat, particularly in patients already at increased risk of
erosions and gastrointestinal bleeding.
In summary, in contrast to the NDD population, we could identify a risk mitigation strategy
(labeling, including a Boxed Warning, Warnings and Precautions, and a Medication Guide) and
post-marketing approach that will allow for the safe administration of daprodustat at the
proposed daily dosing regimen to ensure that the benefits of daprodustat outweigh its risks.
Therefore, we are approving daprodustat for the DD population.
(b) (4)

14
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)
(b) (4)

Additional Considerations
The Applicant should consider exploratory analyses, if they haven’t already, to try to identify
patient factors that predicted thrombosis and thromboembolic events to inform the design and
analysis of future trial(s).
The Applicant should be encouraged to study daprodustat in patients who are considered to have
a poor response to an ESA, since such patients may have a different benefit/risk, given lack of
alternative therapies.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
The Applicant, GlaxoSmithKline, Inc., is seeking approval of daprodustat under the 505(b)(1)
regulatory pathway, for the treatment of anemia associated with chronic kidney disease in adults
on dialysis and not on dialysis. Daprodustat is a new molecular entity, and if approved would be
the first marketed drug in the class of hypoxia-inducible factor prolyl-hydroxylase inhibitors
(HIF-PHI). Two other members of this class, ruxodustat and vadadustat, received Complete
Response letters because of serious risks. Daprodustat is administered orally as opposed to the
1 U.S. Renal Data System, USRDS 2016 Annual Data Report: Atlas of Chronic Kidney Disease and End-Stage

Renal Disease in the United States, National Institutes of Health, National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and
Kidney Diseases, Bethesda, MD, 2016.

15
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

erythropoietin stimulating agents (ESAs), which are administered intravenously or
subcutaneously and are the current standard-of-care treatments for anemia of chronic kidney
disease (CKD).
Daprodustat stimulates endogenous erythropoietin production by stabilizing the hypoxiainducible factor (HIF)-α subunit, allowing it to dimerize with the HIF-β subunit and to stimulate
genes involved in protection against hypoxia, including the erythropoietin gene. HIF-PHI also
influence iron homeostasis through effects on transferrin, transferrin receptor expression,
hepcidin, and other iron-related proteins (Singh et al. 2021). The starting dose of daprodustat is
based on the patient’s dialysis status and hemoglobin level and for those switching from an
erythropoiesis stimulating agent (ESA) to daprodustat, the dose is based on their existing ESA
dose. The dose ranges from 1 to 24 mg orally daily with or without food.
CKD affects 8% to 16% of the population worldwide and is a leading cause of death (Chen et al.
2019). The estimated prevalence of CKD in the U.S. adult population is 15%, with an estimated
17 million people having CKD stages 3 (mild to moderate renal impairment, estimated
glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) 30 to 59 mL/min/1.73 m2) to stage 5 (severe renal impairment,
kidney failure; eGFR <15 mL/min/1.73 m2). The prevalence of anemia increases as the GFR
declines (Hsu 2002; Meier-Kriesche et al. 2002). An estimated 90% of patients requiring dialysis
are anemic.
Anemia is a common complication in CKD and is associated with a reduced quality of life, and
increased morbidity and mortality. The mechanisms involved in anemia associated with CKD are
diverse and complex. They include a decrease in endogenous erythropoietin (EPO) production,
absolute and/or functional iron deficiency, and inflammation with increased hepcidin levels,
among others. Patients are most commonly managed with red blood cell (RBC) transfusions, oral
or intravenous iron supplementation and with ESAs.
ESAs are a class of recombinant glycoproteins that have an identical or nearly identical primary
amino acid sequence to endogenous human erythropoietin and have the same biological effects
as erythropoietin. ESAs bind to and activate the human erythropoietin receptor and stimulate
RBC production in bone marrow. ESA use for the treatment of anemia due to CKD in patients on
dialysis and not on dialysis has spanned over 30 years.
Approved ESAs include Epogen/Procrit (epoetin alfa), Aranesp (darbepoetin alfa), Mircera
(methoxy polyethylene glycol-epoetin beta) and Retacrit (epoetin alfa-epbx); all of which are
approved for the treatment of anemia in patients who are non-dialysis dependent (NDD) and
those who are dialysis dependent (DD) (see Table 3).
Table 3. US-Licensed ESAs for the Treatment of Anemia Due to Chronic Kidney Disease
Product Name Established Name (Trade Name)
Year of Approval
Epoetin alfa (Epogen/Procrit)

Methoxy polyethylene glycol-epoetin beta (Mircera)

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

After the first ESA approval for patients with CKD in 1989, the ESA labeling has undergone
significant revisions because of risks that emerged in the postmarketing setting and in subsequent
clinical trials (summarized below). These labeling revisions have included the following
additions:





A Boxed Warning for increased mortality and serious cardiovascular (myocardial
infarction [MI] and stroke) and thromboembolic events. Of note, there is no identified Hb
target value, ESA dose or dosing strategy that does not increase these risks.
Warnings for hypertension, seizures, and thrombotic events including vascular access
thromboses.
In the dosing and administration section, a reduction in the recommended “target Hb,”
and a recommendation to discontinue the ESA in patients in whom Hb does not respond
adequately over a 12-week dose-escalation period.
Inclusion of other major adverse reactions of ESAs including hypertension, seizures, and
pure red cell aplasia.

ESA use in patients with CKD increases the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events
(MACE). Clinical trials have established that targeting higher rather than lower Hb levels further
increases the risk of MACE, although the ideal Hb target that best balances benefits and risks has
not been identified for any of the ESAs.




The US Normal Hematocrit trial (4) was the first in a series of randomized, controlled
trials designed to test the hypothesis that a higher target hematocrit in subjects receiving
hemodialysis would result in improved outcomes. In this trial, 1233 patients with EndStage Renal Disease (ESRD) on hemodialysis with symptomatic heart failure or ischemic
heart disease were randomized to either partial treatment of anemia (hematocrit of
30±3%) or full correction (hematocrit of 42±3%). The primary endpoint was time to
death or first nonfatal MI. The trial was terminated at the third interim analysis for futility
and potential harm in the full anemia correction group. There were 202 primary endpoint
events in the full correction group compared to 164 events in the partial correction group:
risk ratio 1.3 (95% confidence interval [CI] 0.9, 1.9). Also, 39% of subjects in the full
anemia correction group had vascular access thrombosis compared to 29% of subjects in
the partial treatment arm.
The CHOIR study (5) was a randomized, active-controlled clinical trial in patients with
NDD-CKD that aimed to show superiority of full anemia correction by ESA
administration in terms of cardiovascular (CV) events and death. In this trial, 1432
patients with CKD and anemia (Hb <11 g/dL) received epoetin alfa and were randomly
assigned to a target Hb of either 13.5 g/dL or 11.3 g/dL. The primary endpoint was a
composite of death, MI, hospitalization for heart failure, or stroke. The study was
prematurely stopped for futility after an interim analysis at a median study duration of 16
months because it was considered unlikely that benefit would be demonstrated for the
primary composite CV endpoint. There were 125 events among 715 subjects in the highHb group versus 97 events among 717 subjects in the low-Hb group (hazard ratio [HR],
1.34; 95% CI 1.03, 1.74; [P=0.03]), with death and hospitalization for heart failure
accounting for 75% of the events.

17
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)





The CREATE study (6) in 603 patients with CKD stages 3 to 5 (26% with diabetes)
failed to demonstrate the superiority of full anemia correction (Hb target 13.0 to
15.0 g/dL) with respect to CV events, as compared to partial correction of anemia (Hb
target 11.0 to 12.5 g/dL), when starting ESA therapy at an earlier stage than ESRD.
Subsequently, TREAT, by far the largest trial, examined CV and renal outcomes in 4038
patients with stages 3 and 4 CKD (7). TREAT was the only large placebo-controlled
study to assess CV outcomes. Patients received either darbepoetin alfa to achieve a Hb
target of 13.0 g/dL or matching placebo with rescue darbepoetin alfa when the Hb
concentration was <9.0 g/dL. The HR for the first coprimary endpoint, a composite of
death or a CV event, was 1.05 (95% CI 0.94, 1.17). The HR for the second coprimary
endpoint, death or ESRD, was 1.06 (P=NS). There was, however, a nearly two-fold
increased risk of stroke (HR 1.92; 95% CI 1.38, 2.68) with darbepoetin alfa compared to
placebo. In addition, venous thromboembolic events occurred at a significantly higher
incidence in the darbepoetin alfa arm (2.0%) compared to the placebo arm (1.1%,
P=0.02).

As a result of these concerns, FDA convened the Cardiovascular and Renal Drugs Advisory
Committee in 2010 to discuss whether the ESA indication for the treatment of anemia due to
chronic kidney disease should be withdrawn in the NDD patient population. Votes were strongly
in favor of continued marketing: 1 “yes”, 15 “no”, and 1 abstention.
Current Clinical Practice
The international 2012 Kidney Disease Improving Global Outcomes Clinical Practice Guideline
for the Evaluation and Management of Anemia and Chronic Kidney Disease (8) recommends
addressing all correctable causes of anemia (including iron deficiency and inflammatory states)
prior to initiation of ESA therapy. The guideline recommends balancing the potential benefits of
reducing blood transfusions and improving anemia-related symptoms against the risks of harm in
individual patients (e.g., stroke, vascular access loss, hypertension) (recommendation 1B). For
adults with NDD-CKD and Hb concentrations <10.0 g/dL, the decision whether to initiate ESA
therapy should be individualized based on the rate of fall of Hb concentration, prior response to
iron therapy, the risk of needing a transfusion, the risks related to ESA therapy, and the presence
of symptoms attributable to anemia (recommendation 2C). For adults with NDD-CKD and Hb
concentrations ≥10.0 g/dL, ESA therapy is not recommended (recommendation 2D). For adults
with stage 5 CKD on dialysis, ESA therapy is recommended when Hb is between 9.0 and
10.0 g/dL (recommendation 2B), and the Kidney Disease Improving Global Outcomes Guideline
advises against use to maintain Hb above 11.5 g/dL (recommendation 2C).

3.2. Approach to Clinical Review
The early phase studies, summarized in Section 12, allowed the determination of bioavailability,
bioequivalence, food-effect, pharmacokinetic (PK), and pharmacodynamic (PD) characteristics
of several drug formulations and dosing approaches, in addition to providing preliminary safety
and tolerability data. Review of this portion of the drug development plan was addressed by the
clinical pharmacology team.
Table 4 provides an overview of the late phase clinical trials conducted to support the benefitrisk assessment of daprodustat (the ASCEND program). Overall, these trials were similar in
study design and statistical methods (particularly the cardiovascular outcome trials (CVOTs),
ASCEND-D and ASCEND-ND) but had different eligibility criteria. The primary statistical team
reviewed data and analyses for efficacy outcomes of the individual phase 3 trials, the safety
statistical team reviewed safety data and analyses for adjudicated safety events, and the clinical
team reviewed the study design and provided interpretation of the statistical findings. The
clinical team, with assistance from the clinical data scientist (who furnished standardized safety
reports) reviewed other safety data.

24 mg is the maximum recommended once daily dose.

Selection of Dosing Regimen for the Phase 3 Trials
Based on an integrated analysis of Phase 1 and 2 longitudinal Hb data, a modeling and
simulation approach was taken to select the starting doses and dose levels for once-daily
administration of daprodustat in the global Phase 3 trials. The Applicant developed a mixed
effect population model, which included dose, PK exposure, and time as key independent
variables, and Hb as a repeatedly measured response variable. The dynamics (relationship of
hemoglobin with respect to time) of red blood cell production and lifespan was modeled using 12
transit compartments (see Section 14.5.2), where production rates were influenced by baseline
Hb and daprodustat. After model validation, clinical trial simulations were used to assess the
average Hb and the % of time within the target range of 10 to 11 g/dL over an evaluation period
from Week 28 to 52, as planned in the Phase 3 studies.

7.6.1.8. Adverse Events of Special Interest (Serious)
FDA considers thrombosis (CVA, MI), thromboembolism (including VAT), and heart failure
adverse events of special interest (see Section 7.2). Accordingly, these events were studied as a
primary analysis (MACE) as well as post-hoc supportive analyses for individual components
(see Sections 7.6.1.1 and 7.6.1.2).
The Agency also considered non-adjudicated endpoints including proliferative retinopathy,
macular degeneration, pulmonary artery hypertension, exacerbation of rheumatoid arthritis,
worsening hypertension, seizures, malignancy, and gastrointestinal erosions as AESI, since these
are known risks for ESAs (see Section 7.2). or suspected risks based on nonclinical data (see
Section 7.1).
The blinded adjudication process, while ensuring unbiasedness in the classification and counting
of reported events, may ignore events which are clinically important in our assessment of risk,
but not fulfilling prespecified criteria in the Clinical Events Committee charter. Therefore, we
considered both investigator SAEs and adjudicated events in our assessment of harm.
Additionally, qualitative aspects were collected in the adjudication process which provide
additional insights into the severity and seriousness of counted events and associated treatment
differences. We have displayed results for adjudicated events along with investigator reported
SAE counts for related MedDRA terms and/or queries related to them in Table 80.
This frequency-based analysis demonstrated that:









Hospitalization for Heart Failure: adjudicated events and investigator reported SAEs (the
seriousness implies hospitalization was substantially involved) have good concordance
for measures of absolute and relative risks.
Myocardial Infarction: adjudicated events and investigator reported SAEs have good
concordance for measures of absolute and relative risks. Based on qualitative measures
provided by blinded adjudicators, the severity of MI seems somewhat milder than
epidemiological reports (i.e., a lower proportion of events in ASCEND-ND were
classified as STEMI or fatal). However, there are limitations of cross study analyses (e.g.,
the populations or methods of ascertainment are different) limiting definitive conclusions
on the degree of risk.
Cerebrovascular Accident: adjudicated events and investigator reported SAEs have good
concordance for measures of relative risks. There seems to be a slightly higher absolute
risk of investigator reported stroke events in the daprodustat arm compared to ESA. This
suggests that the absolute measure of harm for clinically important, but non-adjudicated
stroke events may be higher than what adjudicated endpoints alone suggest.
Thromboembolic Events: both SAEs and adjudicated events were dominated by VAT.
Measures of relative risks were reasonably concordant. There was only one fatal
adjudicated TEE event.
Worsening Hypertension and Seizure: serious adverse events were reported in similar
proportions of patients treated with daprodustat and ESA comparator.
Gastrointestinal Erosions/Bleeding: imbalances not favoring daprodustat were seen in all
nSMQs for hemorrhage, perforation, and ulcerations.
139

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)



Retinopathies: serious reports of retinopathies were reported in more subjects treated with
daprodustat (n=9, 0.5%) than darbepoetin (n=5, 0.3%), however the frequency was very
low, limiting conclusions.

Nonclinical findings relating to cyst progression in autosomal dominant polycystic kidney
disease (ADPKD) for another HIF-PHI, roxadustat (FG-2216), were published in the literature
during the conduct of the studies for daprodustat. There is a theoretical risk that the effects seen
with another HIF-PHI, FG-2216 (Fibrogens HIF-PHI), in the mouse model of ADPKD may be
relevant to humans. In Study ASCEND-ND, 122 patients were identified as having ADPKD (65
on daprodustat and 57 on darbepoetin alfa). There was no indication of an accelerated decline in
eGFR in daprodustat treated patients compared with darbepoetin alfa in patients with ADPKD.
All the safety signals discussed here, besides seizure, hypertension, and retinopathies, were key
safety review issues and are discussed in more detail in Section 7.7.

7.6.1.9. Laboratory Findings
Clinically relevant laboratory abnormalities in the NDD population are presented in sections
pertaining to the key safety review issues of the respective organ system. Specifically, liver
biochemistries are presented in Section 7.7. Table 81 shows other pertinent laboratory
abnormalities for laboratory values sufficiently collected over the clinical trial. There is a high
baseline rate of electrolyte abnormalities in CKD patients, which makes it challenging to resolve
treatment-emergent treatment differences.
There were no clinically significant differences between trial arms in relation to the change
values of laboratory parameters from baseline to end of treatment values. We did not find any
credible treatment differences.

7.6.1.10. Vital Signs’ Analyses
Hypertension is a known risk for ESAs, and thus are of special interest for daprodustat. Vitals for
SBP, DBP, and HR were systematically collected during the study. One measurement of SBP,
DBP, and HR was taken with the subject in a semi-supine or seated position after a 5-minute rest
period. Triplicate measurements were taken at baseline and the end of study visit (or early
treatment discontinuation visit, as applicable).
We graphically inspected aggregate plots vs. time and maximum on-treatment vs. baseline values
for each and did not identify trends. One limitation of the SBP and DBP data is that they were
collected using cuffs, which are not as definitive as ambulatory blood pressure monitoring.

antihypertensive agents was stable throughout the treatment period for both treatment arms, and
was balanced between arms, consistent with trends seen in investigator adverse event reports.

7.6.2. Safety Results, ASCEND-D
7.6.2.1. MACE
7.6.2.1.1. Overview
Results for the prespecified primary analysis of time to first MACE are shown in Table 83. The
HR (95% CI) for the primary analysis (on-study analysis) for time to first MACE was 0.93 (0.81,
1.07) comparing daprodustat to ESA. The upper bound of the 95% CI was lower than the
prespecified risk margin of 1.25, therefore the study ruled out the risk margin for MACE.
Kaplan-Meier survival curves for MACE overlapped during the follow-up period (Figure 31).
The IRD was -0.8 events/100 PY (95% CI -2.4, 0.8) between daprodustat and ESA. Incident
events of the MACE composite endpoint (daprodustat 374/1487 [11.1 events/100 PY], ESA
394/1477 [11.9 events/100 PY]) comprised mostly all-cause mortality (daprodustat 244/374,
ESA 233/394), followed by nonfatal MI (daprodustat 101/374, ESA 126/394) and nonfatal stroke
(daprodustat 29/374, ESA 35/394).
Because both co-primary endpoints (mean change in Hb and time to first MACE) ruled out their
respective risk margins, the principal secondary endpoints were tested for superiority by
144
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

comparing the one-sided superiority p-value against the rank-associated alpha derived from the
Holm-Bonferroni method. None of the principal secondary endpoints (MACE, MACE or
thromboembolic events, MACE or HHF, average monthly IV iron dose) were significant.
Tipping point sensitivity analyses to the MACE primary analysis showed that the results (HR
1.08; 95% CI 0.94, 1.25) tipped, i.e., did not rule out the 1.25 risk margin, when censored
subjects in daprodustat arm were assumed to have a (worse) hazard rate of 4.5 times the
observed rate in the daprodustat arm and censored subjects in the ESA arm were assumed to
have a (better) hazard rate of 0.4 times the observed rate in the ESA arm (see Section 17.1). In
summary, the primary analysis results for MACE, assuming non-informative censoring, was
tipped only under extreme assumptions of daprodustat having more MACE and the active
control having less MACE than the observed subjects. Therefore, the primary analysis assuming
non-informative censoring could be relied upon for interpretation.
Competing risk analyses of time to first fatal/nonfatal (a) stroke, (b) MI, and (c) TEE with allcause mortality as the competing event produced estimates that were similar to the primary
analyses that censored subjects at death (results not shown). Competing risk analysis of these
endpoints did not change the interpretation of their respective primary analysis.

7.6.2.1.2. On-Study vs. On-Treatment Analyses
The pre-specified primary analysis of MACE was on-study (HR 0.93; CI 0.81, 1.07). The prespecified supportive OT analysis (OT + 28) of MACE (HR 0.96; CI 0.81, 1.14) was consistent
with the primary on-study analysis (Table 84). Exploratory OT analyses adjusted by the dosing
frequencies of the different treatments were also consistent with the primary on-study analysis.

7.6.2.1.3. Subgroup Analyses

estimates were less than or equal to 1.03 and generally consistent with the overall study

7.6.2.2. CV Endpoints
7.6.2.2.1. Overview
To better understand CV risk, adjudicated CV endpoints, not including all-cause mortality, were
assessed. Results of the primary (on-study) time-to-event analysis of CV endpoints in ASCENDD are shown in Figure 33. A slightly higher incidence of HHF was observed in the daprodustat
arm (112/1487 [3.3 events/100 PY]) compared to the ESA arm (101/1477 [3.0 events/100 PY])
corresponding to a HR of 1.10 (95% CI 0.84, 1.45). A higher incidence of HHF was also
observed in the daprodustat arm in ASCEND-ND (Section 7.6.1.2). A higher incidence was not
observed for other adjudicated CV endpoints in the daprodustat arm. The corresponding IRDs
(per 100-person years) ranged from -1.1 to 0.3 (Appendix Section 17.6).

7.6.2.2.2. Hospitalization for Heart Failure Subgroup
Analyses
Exploratory subgroup analyses by history of heart failure (Yes, No) showed a higher incidence
of HHF in the daprodustat arm compared to the control arm in the subgroup with a history of
heart failure (Table 85). The HR for the subgroup with history of heart failure was 1.44 (95% CI
0.97, 2.14). In contrast, the HR for the subgroup without a history of heart failure was 0.91 (95%
CI 0.63, 1.32).

7.6.2.4. Deaths
In the ASCEND-D study, all-cause mortality was similar between arms (HR 0.96; CI 0.82, 1.13)
(see Figure 71 for survival curves). There were 294/1487 deaths [8.3 deaths/100 PY] in the
daprodustat arm and 300/1477 deaths [8.6 deaths/100 PY] in the ESA arm (Table 87). The
incidence of death from cardiovascular causes was lower in subjects who received daprodustat.
Death from undetermined cause was higher with daprodustat. This is an opposite trend to what
was seen in the non-dialysis population.

7.6.2.5. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Table 88 provides a frequency-based comparison of investigator-reported SAE occurrence, by
SOC and preferred term. Table 89 provides a frequency-based comparison of SAE occurrence by
narrow FMQ. Treatment emergent serious adverse events in the ITT population are reported if
they occurred in at least 1% of subjects in either arm.
The nFMQs for hyperglycemia and bacterial infection showed a treatment difference not
favoring daprodustat. The hyperglycemia query was driven by diverse sequalae of diabetes (e.g.,
foot infections, neuropathies), and was not corroborated by reports of new onset diabetes.
The SOC for infections and infestations were balanced overall, however, there were several PTs
which showed treatment differences not favoring daprodustat, including peritonitis, dialysis
access infections (including "Device related infection" and "Arteriovenous fistula site
infection"), osteomyelitis. When considering nFMQs related to organism or location (e.g., viral,
bacterial, respiratory, integumentary), no common themes in treatment differences was observed.
Importantly, reports of serious sepsis and shock were balanced between treatment arms.
The GI hemorrhage PT was somewhat more notable because the signal was also seen in
ASCEND-ND and was a prespecified AESI. This is discussed in Section 7.7.
We did not identify any other safety signals not favoring daprodustat. In contrast to ASCENDND, cardiovascular endpoints, such as thrombosis and thromboembolisms were favoring
daprodustat in this population.

7.6.2.7. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Table 91 provides a frequency-based comparison of common TEAEs reported in the safety
population during the on-study period for the DD population. This table includes AEs occurring
in greater than 5% in any arm, and with a nominal risk difference (in either direction, favoring or
disfavoring daprodustat) of 1%. You may note that there are more PTs and FMQs that are
included in this table than that of the ND study: this is because these patients are more comorbid
and as a result there is a higher number of patients reporting at least one event and events
reported overall (see Sections 7.6.1.3 and 7.6.2.3) rather than an incongruous reporting method.
The frequency-based analysis demonstrated that:





Thrombotic and thromboembolic events (including MI, CVA) had a nominal
treatment difference favoring daprodustat, which is consistent with the statistical analyses
of adjudicated endpoints discussed in Section 7.4. These AEs are discussed in detail in
Section 7.6.1.8 and Section 7.7 as major review issues.
Nonspecific gastrointestinal related AEs (abdominal pain and dyspepsia nFMQs) were
reported in more subjects treated with daprodustat. These will be included in the label.
Dizziness, falls, and hypotension (FMQ) were more frequently reported in daprodustat
subjects compared to rhEPO, with the risk difference ranging from 1.2 to 2.8% (over a
median on-study duration of approximately 26 months). Hypotension can occur during
dialysis and whether the dizziness, falls and hypotension are interrelated is difficult to
determine. The adverse event of dizziness will be included in the adverse reaction table
of the label.

We did not note any clinically significant differences between study arms in other nonserious
adverse events.

7.6.2.8. Adverse Events of Special Interest
We took a similar approach to the DD population as was done for the NDD population for AESI.
(7.6.1.8 for a discussion of the approach). Table 92 shows a frequency-based listing of
adjudicated events along with related investigator-reported SAEs as MedDRA groupings,
preferred terms, or queries. This frequency-based analysis demonstrated that:




Adjudicated events (MI, CVA, TEE, and HHF): adjudicated events and investigator
reported SAEs have reasonable concordance for measures of absolute and relative risks.
Worsening Hypertension and Seizure: adverse events were reported in similar
proportions of patients treated with daprodustat and ESA comparator.
Gastrointestinal erosions/bleeding: in contrast to ASCEND-ND, harms were not evident
in narrow SMQs for perforation, and ulcerations.
157

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)



Retinopathies: serious reports of retinopathies were reported in more subjects treated with
daprodustat (n=12, 0.8%) than darbepoetin (n=3, 0.2%), however the frequency was low.

ADPKD was reported in 5% of patients overall. As this population is already on dialysis,
progression of renal disease due to ADPKD was not assessed.
Worsening of hypertension is an important risk of ESAs, which includes a section in warnings
and precautions, and a contraindication in patients with uncontrolled hypertension. The
Applicant prespecified an AESI query (narrow SMQ for Hypertension). Serious worsening
hypertension was reported in 3.1% of patients in either arm. The nonserious events were
sufficiently balanced between arms to not be included in table 91 (i.e., there was less than a 1%
risk difference). However, for labeling purposes, the TEAESI (narrow SMQ, serious and
nonserious) was reported in 351 (24%) of patients treated with daprodustat, and 348 (23.6%) of
patients treated with ESA comparator, with event rates of 12 per 100 PY for both (HR: 0.98,
95% CI [0.87 – 1.09]). The PT “Hypertension” dominated the query results (~85%). The
estimates were similar for broad and narrow FMQs and SMQs. Worsening hypertension is an
adverse event of special interest and similar results were observed between daprodustat and ESA
comparator. We recommend including hypertension in the warnings and precautions of the
prescribing information.
All the safety signals discussed here, besides seizure, hypertension, and retinopathy were major
review issues and are discussed in more detail in Section 7.7

7.6.2.9. Laboratory Findings
Table 93 shows other laboratory abnormalities for laboratory values routinely collected in
ASCEND-D. There is a high baseline rate of electrolyte abnormalities in CKD patients, which
makes it challenging to resolve treatment-emergent treatment differences.
There were no clinically significant differences between trial arms in relation to the change
values of laboratory parameters from baseline to end of treatment values. We did not find any
credible treatment differences.

7.6.2.10. Vital Signs’ Analyses
Hypertension is a known risk for ESAs, and thus are of special interest for daprodustat. Vitals for
SBP, DBP, and HR were systematically collected during the study. One measurement of SBP,
DBP, and HR was taken with the subject in a semi-supine or seated position after a 5-minute rest
period. Triplicate measurements were taken at baseline and the end of study visit (or early
treatment discontinuation visit, as applicable).

We graphically inspected aggregate plots vs. time and maximum on-treatment vs. baseline values

controlled for baseline hypertension status. Nonetheless, the objective measurement of
hypertension appears similar between daprodustat and ESA comparator. The use of
antihypertensive agents was stable throughout the treatment period for both treatment arms, and
was balanced between arms, consistent with trends seen in investigator adverse event reports.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Difference Between On-Study Versus OnTreatment Analyses Results – ASCEND-ND
Issue
In ASCEND-ND, the prespecified supportive OT analysis (OT+28) resulted in a higher
incidence of MACE in the daprodustat arm compared to the control arm and an HR estimate (HR
1.40; 95% CI 1.17, 1.68) that was greater than the on-study analysis estimate (HR 1.03; 95% CI
0.89, 1.19).

163
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

Background
To assess risks, two approaches were considered: an on-study analysis and OT analysis. An onstudy analysis considers a subject at risk of experiencing an event from the time of
randomization until trial discontinuation, which includes events that occur while a subject is on
treatment and off treatment (for those subjects who prematurely discontinued treatment but
remained in the trial). An OT analysis considers a subject at risk of experiencing an event from
the time of randomization to the time of treatment discontinuation plus a fixed pre-specified
period of time thereafter (e.g., 28 days).
An on-study analysis approach is valuable in the assessment of risks that have a long latency
period. In addition, such analyses preserve the integrity of randomization. A limitation of an onstudy analysis approach is that it may be less sensitive than the OT analysis to detecting true
adverse effects of the study drug, especially in cases of high rates or between-group imbalances
of premature treatment discontinuation (these subjects who remain in the trial are still considered
at risk for adverse reactions in the analysis but are no longer taking study drug) or use of rescue
medication (that carry their own risks).
An OT analysis approach is sensitive to risks thought to be pharmacodynamic responses to the
drug, and as such, the risk would be expected to occur while exposed to treatment or shortly
thereafter. However, the OT analysis approach has the potential to break the integrity of
randomization and introduce bias as there may be differences between treatment arms in the
types of subjects who adhere to treatment. Such differences cannot easily be adjusted by
statistical analysis.
Assessment
In the development program for daprodustat, an on-study analysis approach was the prespecified
primary analysis and an OT analysis approach was a supportive analysis. Three variations of the
OT analysis accounting for differing treatment dosing intervals, resulting in different
ascertainment windows, were considered (see Section 7.6.1.1.2 and Table 72).
In ASCEND-ND, the prespecified primary on-study analysis approach captured events from
randomization to study completion/withdrawal (mean follow-up duration 1.9 years). Treatment
discontinuation was similar between treatment arms (daprodustat 38%, darbepoetin alfa 38%), as
was study withdrawal (daprodustat 3%, darbepoetin alfa 3%) and use of rescue therapies
(daprodustat 2%, darbepoetin alfa 3%).
The prespecified primary analysis of MACE was on-study (HR 1.03; 95% CI 0.89, 1.19; see
Section 7.6.1.1.1). The prespecified supportive OT analysis (OT+28) resulted in a higher
incidence of MACE in the daprodustat arm compared to the control arm and an HR estimate (HR
1.40; 95% CI 1.17, 1.68) that was greater than the on-study analysis estimate (Table 72).
Exploratory OT analyses adjusted by the dosing frequencies of the different treatments
attenuated the effect estimate; the extent of attenuation depended on the ascertainment window
chosen (OT+Dosing Frequency+28: HR 1.18 (95% CI 0.99, 1.40) and OT+Dosing Frequency:
HR 1.09 (95% CI 0.89, 1.33)).

164
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

Conclusion
In summary, OT analyses produced HR estimates for MACE that were greater than the on-study
analysis estimate, and the OT estimates differed depending on the ascertainment window used.
However, as noted above, comparisons based on the OT analyses are likely subject to bias,
which cannot easily be corrected through analysis. The design and conduct of ASCEND-ND
appeared sufficient to evaluate and estimate risks based on an on-study analysis approach.

7.7.2. Higher Incidence of Cardiovascular Endpoints –
ASCEND-ND
Issue
In ASCEND-ND, the estimated HRs for CV MACE, CV mortality, MI, stroke, HHF, TEE, and
VAT are all increased for the comparison of daprodustat to darbepoetin alfa, ranging from 1.06
to 1.49.
Background
A limitation to the use of MACE to assess CV risk was that MACE was a composite endpoint
prespecified to contain all-cause mortality, therefore non-CV deaths were included. To better
understand CV risk, secondary CV endpoints and CV MACE (a composite of CV mortality,
nonfatal MI, and nonfatal stroke) were analyzed.
Assessment

Results of the primary (on-study) time-to-event analysis of CV endpoints in ASCEND-ND are
shown in Figure 29 and in Table 96. As shown in the figure and table, the estimated HRs for CV
MACE, CV mortality, MI, stroke, HHF, TEE, and VAT are all increased for the comparison of
daprodustat to darbepoetin alfa, ranging from 1.06 to 1.49. The corresponding IRDs (per 100person years) ranged from 0.2 to 0.7.
The review team also carefully considered if the thrombotic and thromboembolic risks observed
in ASCEND-ND could be mitigated using a population selection approach. Population selection
is the most feasible mitigation measure, given thrombosis/thromboembolism does not typically

exploratory analyses were conducted for each adjudicated CV endpoint in the USA and nonUSA subgroups.
Assessment
A higher incidence of each CV endpoint was observed in the daprodustat arm compared to the
darbepoetin alfa arm in the USA subgroup (Figure 30). Except stroke, the HRs of the non-USA
subgroup analyses were approximately 1.0, and the HRs of the USA subgroup analyses were
elevated (ranging from 1.21 to 2.03). The variability (95% CI width) of HR estimates depended
on the number of events and sample sizes, with lower precision for the USA subgroup compared
to the non-USA subgroup and for endpoints like stroke and TEE.
There were differences in baseline characteristics of the USA compared to the non-USA
subgroup. USA subjects used less ESAs at baseline, which was also reflected in baseline Hb
category, and generally had more comorbid conditions at baseline (history of diabetes, heart
failure, cardiovascular disease). In addition, there were differences in enrollment by race
between USA and non-USA subgroups (Table 97). It is unclear if these differences contributed
to the discrepant results between the USA and non-USA subgroups.

Conclusion
Although exploratory analyses could introduce bias and produce unreliable results, the higher
risk estimates in the USA subgroup compared to the non-USA subgroup in the NDD population
were seen across multiple CV endpoints and were consistent with the unfavorable MACE
prespecified region subgroup results (Section 7.6.1.1.3). In summary, these higher incidences of
CV endpoints in the daprodustat arm in the USA subgroup raised concerns as to whether
daprodustat is safe relative to darbepoetin alfa, which itself carries an increased CV risk.

7.7.4. Higher Incidence of Hospitalization for Heart
Failure - ASCEND-ND, ASCEND-D
Issue
A higher incidence of HHF with daprodustat compared to active control was observed in both
ASCEND-ND and ASCEND-D studies. In ASCEND-D, only HHF (among CV endpoints)
showed an increased risk estimate in the daprodustat arm compared to active control. In
ASCEND-ND, HHF with other CV endpoints showed an increased risk estimate in the
daprodustat arm compared to active control.
Background
To explore contributing factors that might have resulted in the HHF imbalance between
treatments, subgroup analyses by history of heart failure were conducted.
Assessment
In ASCEND-ND (Section 7.6.1.2.1), a higher incidence of HHF was observed in the daprodustat
arm (140/1937 [4.0 events/100 PY]) compared to the darbepoetin alfa arm (115/1935
[3.3 events/100 PY]), corresponding to a HR of 1.22 (95% CI 0.95, 1.56). A higher incidence
was also observed for other adjudicated CV endpoints in the daprodustat arm in ASCEND-ND.
In ASCEND-D (Section 7.6.2.2.1), a higher incidence of HHF was observed in the daprodustat
arm (112/1487 [3.3 events/100 PY]) compared to the ESA arm (101/1477 [3.0 events/100 PY])
168
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

corresponding to a HR of 1.10 (CI 0.84, 1.45). A higher incidence was not observed for other
adjudicated CV endpoints in the daprodustat arm.
In both ASCEND-ND and ASCEND-D, exploratory subgroup analyses by history of heart
failure (Yes, No) showed a higher incidence of HHF in the daprodustat arm compared to the
control arm in the subgroup with a history of heart failure (Table 73, Table 85). The HR for the
subgroup with history of heart failure was 1.51 (95% CI 1.01, 2.25) in ASCEND-ND and 1.44
(95% CI 0.97, 2.14) in ASCEND-D. In contrast, the HR for the subgroup without a history of
heart failure was 0.99 (95% CI 0.72, 1.36) in ASCEND-ND and 0.91 (95% CI 0.63, 1.32) in
ASCEND-D.
The observed increased risk for HHF was attenuated in the DD population. Potential
explanations include more frequent fluid management (e.g., dialysis), and more intense
monitoring (e.g., more frequent health care interactions, where eminent heart failure
exacerbations are identified and mitigated).
Conclusion
Limitations of the HHF analyses, including the subgroup analyses by history of HF, include
lower precision (compared to MACE) because of lower event rates (and hence wider 95% CIs)
and no Type I error control for multiple comparisons between treatment arms. However, the
increased risks for HHF were observed in both the NDD and DD populations and in the
subgroup with history of heart failure of both populations. While the precision and post-hoc
nature of this analysis has limitations, there is a plausible mechanism for the subgroup
interaction and an appreciable effect modification observed in both studies. For these reasons, we
concluded that the HHF risk could be reliably mitigated by including a warning for patients with
a history of heart failure in the DD population.

7.7.5. Three Times a Week (TIW) Dosing in ASCENDTD
Issue
ASCEND-TD established non-inferiority of daprodustat TIW to IV epoetin alfa on Hb in the DD
(hemodialysis only) population. However, the study was not designed to demonstrate general or
cardiovascular safety. Is the safety established from ASCEND-D sufficient to establish safety for
a TIW dosing regimen?
Background
Patients on hemodialysis typically have in-center hemodialysis sessions three times a week,
where hemoglobin is routinely monitored and IV epoetin alfa is administered during the
hemodialysis session. A daprodustat TIW dosing regimen may offer patient convenience.
Additionally, three times a week dosing would enable institutionalization of treatment (i.e.,
daprodustat treatment could be managed by the hemodialysis center for patients on
hemodialysis), which may confer enhanced adherence over outpatient-based anemia treatment.
ASCEND-TD was designed to demonstrate efficacy, but not cardiovascular or general safety in
the DD population (n=407, 2:1 randomization to daprodustat: epoetin alfa). Briefly, ASCEND169
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

TD was a randomized, double-blind, active-controlled, double dummy, parallel-group, multicenter study in hemodialysis-dependent patients (refer to Section 6 for additional study design
details). This study demonstrated NI for hemoglobin using a dosing scheme with two times
higher starting dose and titration.
Assessment
The clinical trial data in ASCEND-TD were insufficient to make an independent determination
of safety, particularly CV safety. The results of the adjudicated endpoints are presented in Table
98. ASCEND-TD was a fixed-duration study, and these results represent the proportion of
incident AEs over the 52-week duration. There are small imbalances not favoring daprodustat in
MI (4.1% vs. 3.6%) and stroke (3.0% vs. 0%), and other small imbalances favoring daprodustat
in TEE (10.0% vs. 12.4%) and HHF (1.9% vs. 2.9%). However, given the small sample size and
low event rates with unstable estimates (e.g., only 1 additional MI with rhEPO would eradicate
the MI imbalance), the precision of treatment effect estimates precludes definitive inference.
Therefore, the cardiovascular safety of the TIW regimen in hemodialysis is indeterminate based
on the existing AE data.

Cardiovascular safety is related to hemoglobin levels – as was shown in CHOIR, CREATE,
TREAT and the Normal Hematocrit study. Additionally, it is postulated that Hb rate of rise may
contribute to the pro-thrombotic effects of ESAs, and by extension to HIF-PHIs. The Office of
Clinical Pharmacology reviewed a PK/PD model describing the relationship of daprodustat PK
to hemoglobin concentrations. This model demonstrates that the hemoglobin trajectory versus
time is nearly identical for TIW and once daily (QD) dosing regimens of equivalent
pharmacodynamic potency (2-fold higher doses administered TIW had similar
pharmacodynamic effect to once daily regimens). The treatment differences between QD and
170
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

TIW dosing in Hb fluctuations (i.e., the peak to trough ratio) were minimal, and not clinically
important (see Section 14.5.3.1). Additionally, there are lower systemic erythropoietin levels
observed with HIF-PHIs than with exogenous administration.
However, there may be other mechanisms by which daprodustat can contribute to cardiovascular
harm. There is a worrisome trend in cardiovascular endpoints for daprodustat in the NDD
population that does not have a clear explanation despite similar Hb responses to daprodustat and
darbepoetin alfa in that trial. Therefore, the similar Hb levels with TIW vs. once daily dosing of
equivalent potency do not provide adequate assurance that the TIW dosing regimen will be safe.
TIW dosing results in a Cmax approximately two-fold higher than a once daily regimen of
approximately equivalent pharmacodynamic potency. To explore the effects of Cmax on
cardiovascular safety, we conducted multivariate exposure-response analyses using a logistic
regression model and Cox proportional hazards regression model to quantify the relationship
between daprodustat PK exposures (e.g., average Cmax prior to the occurrence of the first event,
or average on-treatment Cmax for patients without an event) and safety measures
(thromboembolic event, nonfatal MI, and nonfatal hospitalization for HF) by Week 52 based on
data from ASCEND-D and ASCEND-TD. The post-hoc analyses from these Cox and logistical
regression models show Cmax is not a significant factor in any of those safety measures and no
differences between the average Cmax up to the event (in those with the event) vs. the average
Cmax for 52 weeks (in those without the event) were detected (see Section 14.5.3.2). However,
these data were derived from a small subset of patients and events. Additionally, because the
effect of Cmax on safety was based on a post-randomization analysis, the analysis may be subject
to confounding.
We also considered whether safety for TIW could be extrapolated from the once daily safety
database from ASCEND-D (Table 99). For example, a patient who received 24 mg TIW in
ASCEND-TD would be expected to have Cmax similar to those in ASCEND-D receiving 24 mg
daily (the highest studied once daily dose), hence providing support for a 24 mg TIW regimen.

7.7.6. CV Safety in Incident Dialysis
Issue
ASCEND-ID established non-inferiority of daprodustat for efficacy in the ID population.
However, the study was not designed to demonstrate general safety or cardiovascular outcomes.
Is the safety established from ASCEND-D and ASCEND-ND sufficient to establish safety for ID
patients?
Background
The incident dialysis ID population is defined broadly as patients who have recently transitioned
to chronic dialysis, or transition to dialysis is eminent. The ASCEND-ID study defined the ID
population as those who are either planning to start chronic dialysis within the next 6 weeks from
the date of the screening visit (which was 6 weeks prior to randomization) or those who had
started chronic dialysis (hemodialysis or peritoneal dialysis) for no more than 90 days prior to
randomization. Importantly, the inclusion criteria for ASCEND-ND and ASCEND-D ensure
virtually no overlap with the ASCEND-ID population: ASCEND-ND enrolled subjects with
stage 3, 4, or 5 CKD, who did not have clinical evidence of impending need to initiate dialysis
within 90 days after study start, whereas ASCEND-D required participants to have been on
dialysis for greater than 90 days prior to screening (which was at least 4 weeks from
randomization)
ID patients are at an acutely elevated risk for cardiovascular morbidity and mortality.
Additionally, the cardiovascular safety profile in the DD and NDD populations are disparate
(ASCEND-ND being broadly disfavorable to daprodustat, whereas ASCEND-D being broadly
favorable to daprodustat), and the ID population is a transient state between being dialysis
dependent and not dialysis dependent. These disparate results, as well as lack in understanding
the root cause for the cardiovascular differences seen with daprodustat in the NDD vs. DD
populations make reliance (i.e., extrapolation) of the safety profile from one or both populations
to the ID population challenging.
Assessment
Table 101 provides a comparison between daprodustat and rhEPO of the proportion of subjects
experiencing an adjudicated cardiovascular event during the 52-week study period in ASCENDID. Daprodustat had a slightly higher incidence of ACM and HF events, a slightly lower
174

incidence of TEE, and comparable rates for MI and CVA compared to rhEPO. While being
specific to the ID population, this trial was too small (n=312) and short term (52 weeks) and the
number of events of interest were too low to provide precise estimates of risk. Therefore, the
cardiovascular safety of daprodustat in the ID population is indeterminate based on the existing
AE data.

Given this considerable uncertainty regarding the safety of daprodustat in the ID population, we

safety in the ID population. Approximately 35% (1345/3872) of subjects in ASCEND-ND
transitioned to dialysis while on study (i.e., were incident dialysis).
We considered TEAE around the time of dialysis initiation (i.e., the TEAE and dialysis initiation
must occur together, within an arbitrary, post-hoc window) as an endpoint to explore the safety
of ND subjects who transitioned to ID during the course of ASCEND-ND. We chose several
windows for defining a co-occurring event. Table 102 below shows the incidence of SAEs by
treatment arm and by SOC and nFMQ within 30 days before or after dialysis initiation in those
subjects who transitioned to dialysis in ASCEND-ND. There are slightly higher incidences of
thrombosis, myocardial ischemic-type events, and acute kidney injury with daprodustat
compared to rhEPO during this timeframe.

7.7.7. Gastric Erosions and Gastrointestinal Bleeding
Issue
A higher incidence of serious investigator reported treatment emergent AEs related to gastric
erosions and gastrointestinal bleeding was observed in the daprodustat arm compared to the ESA
comparator arm in ASCEND-ND and ASCEND-D.
Background
The Applicant’s nonclinical data demonstrated gastric erosions/ulcerations in mice, rats, dogs,
and monkeys with a possible basis for erosions and ulcers being compromised vascular perfusion
associated with marked increases in hematocrit in non-anemic animals. Potential upper
gastrointestinal erosive events were identified and collected prospectively in the ASCEND-D
and ASCEND-ND trials as an AESI.
The Applicant prespecified a list of MedDRA PTs pertaining to the AESI of gastric erosions.
This list is available publicly19 on page 636 of the protocol provided as supplementary material.
Our review of the individual PTs used in the Applicant’s query determined that the breadth of the
queries was reasonable for a risk specific to gastric erosion. The adverse events were not
adjudicated and diagnostic confirmation with upper endoscopy was not required in the protocols.
Assessment
There was a higher incidence of Applicant-defined AESIs for serious gastric/esophageal erosions
reported in the daprodustat arm compared to ESA with a more notable difference in the
ASCEND-ND population (HR [95% CI]: 1.63 [1.17, 2.27]) than in ASCEND-D (HR [95% CI]:
1.16 [0.78, 1.73]) (Table 104). The incidence of all TEAEs of gastric/esophageal erosions
(serious plus non-serious) was similarly increased with daprodustat compared to ESA in
ASCEND-ND (HR [95% CI]: 1.96 [1.24, 3.09]) but not in ASCEND-D (HR [95% CI]: 0.84
(0.63, 1.13)].

The differences in risk are driven by treatment differences in serious gastrointestinal

serious gastrointestinal hemorrhages are superimposed for the first 24 months of ASCEND-D
and then separate slightly. In contrast, the Kaplan-Meier curves for serious gastrointestinal
hemorrhages in ASCEND-ND separate early. Importantly, the treatment arms in ASCEND-D
and ASCEND-ND were balanced with respect to anti-platelet, anti-coagulant use, prophylactic
drugs (e.g., antiacids), and risk factors (gastroesophageal reflux disease, peptic ulcer disease,
smoking).

The Applicant provided an external, blinded, review of the serious AESIs of gastric or
esophageal erosions from ASCEND-D and ASCEND-ND. This review found that most included
events were overt gastrointestinal bleeding, with over half requiring transfusion. However, the
external blinded review also noted several limitations related to the fact that the AE reports did
not trigger confirmatory testing (e.g., Helicobacter pylori testing and confirmatory endoscopy).

As a potential risk mitigation strategy, we considered known risk factors which were also
reported in the subject level dataset. These included smoking history, use of aspirin, clopidogrel,
and vitamin K antagonists (e.g., warfarin) at baseline. In the pooled ASCEND-D and ASCENDND dataset, aspirin was independently associated with serious gastrointestinal erosions/bleeds
(HR 1.52 [95% CI: 1.1 – 2.1]), as was clopidogrel (HR 1.16 [95% CI: 0.7 – 1.9]), and warfarin
(HR 2.0 [95% CI: 1.1 – 3.4]). Smoking history was not strongly related.
Subgroup analyses for serious gastric erosions/bleeds did not suggest a substantial treatment
interaction with concomitant aspirin, warfarin, or clopidogrel (Table 105). It is important to note
that the warfarin and clopidogrel subgroups were limited in the trial population (less than 10% of
subjects used warfarin or clopidogrel). Approximately one-half used aspirin. These products (as
well as others, such as NSAIDs) may increase the risk of GI bleeding. The data from ASCENDD and ASCEND-ND are too limited to reliably assess the extent of the role these agents played
in the GI bleeding risk with daprodustat.

Conclusion
In summary, there was a treatment difference in serious esophageal and gastric erosions
disfavoring daprodustat that was more pronounced in ASCEND-ND than ASCEND-D. Whether
there is a true risk in the ASCEND-D population is unclear as there are some inconsistencies in
the data. For example, all TEAEs of esophageal and gastric erosions (serious plus non-serious)
did not disfavor daprodustat in ASCEND-D. Also, the risk of serious esophageal and gastric
erosions in the on-study analysis of ASCEND-D was not apparent or became weaker in the OT
analyses. Other limitations include the lack of confirmatory testing, including endoscopy.
Nonetheless, most of these reported serious events were related to gastrointestinal bleeding and
had important clinical consequences such as transfusions and at this time we cannot rule out a
drug effect based on the nonclinical findings and signal in both ASCEND-D and ASCEND-ND
populations. Therefore, we will include a Warning and Precaution in the label, recommending
the healthcare provider consider this risk, particularly in patients who have risk factors for
erosions.

7.7.8. Acute Kidney Injury
Issue
A higher incidence of serious investigator reported treatment emergent AEs related to AKI was
observed in the daprodustat arm compared to the ESA comparator arm in ASCEND-ND.
Background
Direct daprodustat-mediated nephrotoxicity was not observed in the nonclinical program
(although there were effects secondary to non-physiologic Hb levels and blood viscosity, leading
to compromised blood flow and vascular perfusion, including in the kidney). In ASCEND-ND,
the time to first occurrence of CKD progression was a pre-specified principal secondary
endpoint, which was assessed in participants with preserved kidney function at baseline (eGFR
≥15 mL/min/1.73 m2, N=2,485). This endpoint was a composite of a confirmed (through repeat
eGFR) 40% decline in eGFR, clinical indication for dialysis, transition to chronic dialysis, or
receipt of a renal allograft. CKD progression was generally similar between the treatment groups
and daprodustat did not significantly reduce the time to progression of CKD compared with
darbepoetin (IRD [95% CI] -0.2 [-2.8, 2.4] events per 100 PY, HR 0.98, 95% CI: 0.84, 1.13).
Routine safety laboratory results (collected at least every 12 weeks) in ASCEND-ND (e.g.,
aggregate longitudinal plots and shift tables) for serum creatinine, eGFR, and BUN was similar
between treatment groups.
In contrast, the FDA analyses using treatment-emergent serious adverse events (TESAEs) of
AKI shows a potential increased risk for serious AKI for daprodustat compared to the ESA
treatment arm in the NDD population.
Assessment
We performed exploratory analyses to further refine the safety signal of AKI. Adverse event
analyses of serious AKI (as a nFMQ) in ASCEND-ND showed a relative risk of 1.47 (95% CI
1.07, 2.00) and risk difference of 1.54% [0.30, 2.79]). The risks were attenuated when
considering all AKI TEAEs regardless of seriousness (relative risk of 1.2, 95% CI [-0.4, 2.7]).
We note that the treatment difference appears after 16 months on study, so we preferred
cumulative incidence differences to quantify risk. A Kaplan-Meier curve for time to first
treatment-emergent serious adverse event (TESAE) of AKI for the ASCEND-ND study is
presented in Figure 36. At 2 and 3 years, there was a cumulative incidence difference of 2.1%
(95% CI: 0.5, 3.5) and 2.7% (95% CI 0.8, 4.6), respectively.

[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]

Abbreviations: Dapro, daprodustat; ESA, erythropoietin-stimulating agent; FMQ, FDA medical query; ITT, intent-to-treat; TESAE,
treatment-emergent serious adverse event

There was a higher percentage of participants randomized to the daprodustat arm with more
progressed CKD (i.e., stage 4 or 5), and advanced age (≥75-year-old age), which may have
contributed, to some degree, to the observed treatment difference (both are risk factors for AKI).
However, an analysis provided by the Applicant, adjusting for baseline CKD stage, did not
meaningfully change the estimate of harm (Table 46 of advisory committee briefing document).
The Applicant’s key secondary endpoint, by design, does not capture acute AKI events, because
a repeat measurement of eGFR was required to confirm a 40% decline in eGFR. A sensitivity
analysis, which included a single measurement of a 40% decline in eGFR as a CKD progression
event, did capture an unfavorable treatment difference. Lastly, the occurrence of a serious event
is clinically important, regardless of whether it contributed to long term outcomes (such as
progression of CKD or ACM). We acknowledge that the predefined adjudicated secondary
safety analyses such as ACM (HR 1.03, 95% CI: 0.87, 1.20) and CKD progression (HR 0.98,
95% CI: 0.84, 1.13) were balanced between arms.
We considered whether the AKI TESAE imbalance could be related to the increased risk for
HHF with daprodustat compared to darbepoetin alfa. However, the AKI risk was still evident in

185
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

marketed erythropoiesis stimulating agents include an increased risk of tumor progression as a
boxed warning. Whether daprodustat shares this risk of ESA products is uncertain.
Lifetime studies in rodents did not identify a carcinogenic effect of daprodustat. However, based
on the findings with ESAs and the theoretical risk related to the mechanism of action of
daprodustat, the Applicant prespecified malignancy as an AESI. The Applicant’s prespecified
analysis approach for non-CV events was OT, defined by the last dose given plus 1 day. The
ITT/on study analysis is more sensitive than the OT for long-latency events (such as
malignancy), and less confounded by differences in dosing frequency (such as the darbepoetin
alfa comparator in ASCEND-ND)(see Section 7.6.1.1.2).
The ASCEND-D and ASCEND-ND protocols required treatment discontinuation following a
diagnosis of cancer (new or recurrent), with the exception of squamous cell or basal cell
carcinoma of the skin. Therefore, in the on-treatment assessment a malignancy diagnosis that
was made two or more days after the last dose was not counted as an event, because perprotocol, subjects would not be dosed again. This scenario would be expected to exclude more
events occurring with a drug dosed less frequently, but that are still very proximal to randomized
treatment.
Assessment
For the above reasons, we considered the OT analysis as prespecified in the protocol but focused
mainly on the on-study estimates for assessment of malignancy risk. We used a pooled analysis
of ASCEND-ND and ASCEND-D and included treatment-emergent events regardless of
reported seriousness. A summary of these results is presented in Table 108.
The on-study analysis did not suggest a treatment group difference in the incidence of
malignancy (152 of 3419 [4.4%] daprodustat and 161 of 3407 [4.7%] ESA control with a relative
risk of 0.9 (95% CI 0.8, 1.2). There did not appear to be notable imbalances in any pattern type
or location of cancer between treatment groups. Fatal outcomes due to malignancy in both arms
were infrequent.

Estimates of harm specific to the dialysis dependent population are required for the purposes of
product labeling. Serious and nonserious malignancies (prespecified AESI query) were observed
in 4.4% (n=65,) in ASCEND-D treated with daprodustat and 5.2% (n = 77,) of patients treated
with rhEPO. Related queries (i.e., narrow FMQ, narrow SMQ for Malignancy) estimated similar
incidences and treatment differences. The clinical reviewer reproduced the Applicant’s
estimated incidence rates for each treatment arm (1.9 per 100 PY for daprodustat, and 2.3 per
100 PY for subjects treated with ESA), using the submitted time-to-event datasets (adtte3.xpt).
Conclusion
In summary, the risk for tumor progression does not appear to be further increased compared to
currently labeled ESAs, however, the short latency period of the studies conducted to date and
the low event rates for specific cancers do not allow for definite conclusions on the long-term
risk of daprodustat on tumor progression or development of new malignancies. For this reason,
we are requiring a post-marketing observational study to access long term outcomes of
daprodustat on malignancy incidence. In addition, a risk for malignancy will be included in the
warnings and precautions section of the label. Refer to the postmarketing requirement (PMR) in
Section 24 for additional details.

7.7.10. Drug Induced Liver Injury
Issue
We assessed if there was a clinically significant hepatocellular injury risk with the use of
daprodustat in subjects with CKD.
Background
There was no significant hepatotoxicity signal identified by the Applicant during development of
daprodustat. Early during our exploratory review of individual patient data, an imbalance of
potential Hy’s law cases, not favoring daprodustat, was observed in routinely collected study site
labs (pooled ASCEND-D and ASCEND-ND: 4/3424 cases in the daprodustat arm, 2/3412 cases
in the ESA comparator arm). Inspection of mean-level hepatic injury biomarkers versus time
(aspartate aminotransferase, alanine aminotransferase, bilirubin) as well as shift tables did not
reveal a population-wide signal. However, out of an abundance of caution, we consulted the
Drug Induced Liver Injury (DILI) team to assess whether there is a DILI risk with daprodustat
(Refer to DILI review dated November 29, 2022 in DARRTs).
Assessment
Our initial exploratory DILI analysis only included routine, study-site laboratory data. Per
protocol, non-study site lab data was missing from the integrated laboratory dataset (i.e., the
ADLB.xpt dataset). On August 5, 2022, the Applicant submitted an updated ADLB dataset for
each ASCEND study and the Integrated Summary of Safety including central and non-study site
lab results (importantly, the Applicant conducted a manual search in case report forms to locate
non-study site data and update the ADLB dataset, as requested). For all Integrated Summary of
Safety subjects, there were no clear imbalances in incidence of alanine aminotransferase/
aspartate aminotransferase elevations, jaundice, or cases meeting Hy’s Law liver test criteria
between subjects receiving daprodustat versus those receiving rhEPO (Table 109).

eDISH plotting had numerous cases in the Hy’s Law quadrant but no imbalance between

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
