14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
14.1.1. Plasma protein binding
Plasma Protein Binding of Daprodustat
In a study to determine the extent of the binding of daprodustat to plasma proteins from mouse,
rat, rabbit, dog, monkey, and human pooled plasma (3 subjects/pooled sample), plasma samples
were incubated, using equilibrium dialysis, at daprodustat concentrations of 0.2, 1, 10 and
50 µg/mL [Report CD2010/00340]. In addition, the protein binding of daprodustat to human
serum albumin (HSA) (0.600mM) or α1-acid glycoprotein (AAG) (0.015 and 0.150mM) was
determined using equilibrium dialysis at daprodustat concentrations of 0.2, 1, 10 and 30 µg/mL.
The binding of daprodustat to plasma protein was high in all species evaluated (generally >98%),
specifically in human, 99.4% at the three low test concentrations, and 98.3% at 50 µg/mL.
Binding was also high to HSA (>99.3%), while low to AAG (<20%). In general, there was no
evidence of concentration-dependent protein binding of daprodustat in all species over the
concentration range evaluated.
Plasma Protein Binding of Metabolites of Daprodustat
In a study to determine the extent of human plasma protein binding for the six predominant
circulating human metabolites of daprodustat [M2 (GSK2391220), M3 (GSK2531403 and
GSK2506104), M4 (GSK2499166), M5 (GSK2531399), M6 (GSK2531398), and M13
(GSK2531400 and GSK2531401)], pooled human plasma was obtained from three non-fasted
266
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

male donors [2012N131897]. Binding was determined by equilibrium dialysis at concentrations
of 10, 100, 250 and 500 ng/mL and were incubated for 18 hours. Concentrations of each
metabolite were determined by ultra-performance liquid chromatography with tandem mass
spectroscopy (UPLC-MS/MS). The plasma protein binding of M2, M3 (both enantiomers), M6,
and M13 (both enantiomers) was determined to be low, less than 34% for each metabolite across
concentrations. There was no evidence of concentration-dependent protein binding of M2, M3,
M6, and M13 over the range evaluated. The plasma protein binding of M4 and M5 was
determined to be lower (68 and 74%, respectively) at the lowest test concentration (10 ng/mL)
and increased with increasing concentrations up to ~98%, suggesting concentration-dependent
protein binding of M4 and M5 over the concentration range evaluated.

14.1.2. Distribution in red blood cells
The in vitro blood cell association of daprodustat was determined in blood (a single human
plasma sample) by adding daprodustat at concentrations of 1.0 and 10 µg/mL
(n=3/concentration/species, 0.5% (v/v) DMSO) after incubation for 30 minutes. All quantitative
analysis was by LC-MS/MS. There was generally minimal association of daprodustat with blood
cells in vitro with blood/plasma ratios of 1.23 and 0.75 at 1.0 and 10 µg/mL, respectively.

14.1.3. Metabolism studies
Oxidative Enzymology in Human Liver Microsomes
The oxidative enzymology of [14C]daprodustat (5µM) was investigated using pooled human liver
microsomes and rh-CYP enzymes (Supersomes™). Human liver microsomes from mixed-gender
pools of 15 human livers were used. Inhibition of daprodustat metabolism was investigated by
incubating [14C]daprodustat with human liver microsomes in the presence and absence of the
selective CYP inhibitors: azamulin, sulphaphenazole, quinidine, montelukast, furafylline, and
benzylnirvanol (inhibitors of CYP3A4, CYP2C9, CYP2D6, CYP2C8, CYP2C19, CYP1A2,
respectively). Supersomes contained individually over-expressed rh-CYP enzymes (CYP1A2,
CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 and CYP3A4) derived from baculovirus infected insect
cells. [14C]daprodustat incubations with pooled human liver microsomes or recombinant CYP
enzymes were conducted. The reactions were started by the addition of an NADPH regenerating
system. The supernatants from [14C] GSK1278863 incubations with human liver microsomes or
recombinant CYP enzymes were analyzed by radio-HPLC. [14C]daprodustat was metabolized in
human liver microsomes to three metabolites, M8, M9/M22, and M10 (mono-oxidation). An
additional NADPH-dependent radio-peak was detected in incubations with human liver
microsomes. This peak was not detected by LC/MS and therefore was not structurally
characterized, but its rate of formation was calculated. These metabolites, which constituted 95%
of the total radioactivity, were inhibited by montelukast, a selective CYP2C8 inhibitor.
[14C]daprodustat was metabolized to M2, M3, M4, M5, M6, M7, M8, M9/M22, M10, and M17,
in human recombinant CYP2C8 incubations. Metabolites M2 through M7 (di-oxidation) were
not detected in human liver microsomes. In human recombinant CYP3A4 incubations only M8
and M9/M22 were formed but turnover was low and only constituted 5% turnover. There were
no metabolites detected in incubations with recombinant CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19 or
267
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

CYP2D6. The data from human liver microsomes with selective inhibitors and scaled results
from recombinant human CYP enzymes suggest that CYP2C8 is the primary CYP enzyme
involved in the oxidative metabolism of daprodustat in vitro with a minor contribution by
CYP3A4.
Induction of CYP Enzymes in Human Hepatocytes
The effects of daprodustat (0.1, 0.3, 1.0, 3.0, 10, 30 and 100µM) on the mRNA levels of
CYP1A2, 2B6 and 3A4 were evaluated in cultured human hepatocytes from three donors after
exposure to daprodustat for 48 hours. The mRNA level for each specific CYP was determined
using quantitative real-time polymerase chain reaction technology (TaqMan™). No notable
increases in the mean mRNA levels of CYP1A2, CYP2B6 or CYP3A4 were observed. Minor
reductions in mRNA levels (ratio of treated over control <0.5) were apparent for CYP1A2 at
100µM and CYP3A4 at all concentrations tested and the decreases in mRNA level observed may
not be associated with hepatocyte health because no decreases in total RNA and glyceraldehyde
3-phosphate dehydrogenase (GAPDH) were observed. Treatment with daprodustat caused no
notable increases in the mean mRNA levels of CYP1A2, CYP2B6 or CYP3A4 within the
concentration range examined.
Inhibition of CYP Enzymes in Human Microsomes
The potential of daprodustat to inhibit CYP1A2, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6 or 3A4 in a concentrationdependent or metabolism dependent manner was investigated in vitro with human liver
microsomes and using selective probe substrates in the presence and absence of daprodustat (0.1
to 100µM). For concentration-dependent inhibition (half maximal inhibitory concentration [IC50]
determination), daprodustat (0.1 to 100µM) was pre-incubated with human liver microsomal
protein and the respective probe substrates (at a final concentration approximating the Km) with
NADPH (NADP+, glucose-6-phosphate and glucose 6-phosphate dehydrogenase) generating
system was added to start the reactions. The selective probe substrates used in the presence and
absence of daprodustat were: hydroxylation of phenacetin (1A2); 4-hydroxylation of diclofenac
(2C9); 4-hydroxylation of S-mephenytoin (2C19); 1-hydroxylation of bufuralol (2D6); 1hydroxylation of midazolam, oxidation of nifedipine and o-hydroxylation of atorvastatin (3A4).
The reactions to measure metabolism-dependent inhibition were carried out in the presence and
absence of NADPH.
The potential for daprodustat to inhibit the major human CYPs was low in human liver
microsomes with all IC50 values >100µM. In addition, daprodustat did not display any
metabolism-dependent inhibition in human liver microsome CYP1A2, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6 or 3A4nifedipine assays.

14.1.4. Transporter interaction studies
P-glycoprotein Transport and Membrane Permeability
P-gp Substrate Potential and Permeability
The P-gp substrate activity and intrinsic apparent permeability of daprodustat were evaluated in
Madin Darby canine kidney (MCDK) cells transfected with the human multi-drug resistant 1
268
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

gene (hMDR1) [Report UH2008/00018]. Bidirectional [apical-to-basolateral (A→B) and
basolateral-to-apical (B→A)] transport of daprodustat (5µM) was studied across MDCKIIhMDR1 cell monolayers in the absence and presence of GF120918 (P-gp inhibitor at 2µM). The
apical efflux ratio was defined as the ratio of unidirectional transmonolayer flux of test
compound across the cell monolayers from the basolateral to apical (B→A) compartment
divided by that of the same compound from the A→B compartment. An apical efflux ratio >2 for
a compound suggests P-gp-mediated transport. Daprodustat was not a substrate of human P-gp in
vitro, as the apical efflux ratio was ~1 in the absence or presence of GF120918 in MDCKIIhMDR1 cell monolayers.
Passive Membrane Permeability
A study was conducted to determine the passive membrane permeability and the absorptive
membrane permeability of daprodustat using bio-relevant buffer [fasted state simulated intestinal
fluid (FaSSIF)] in MDCKII-hMDR1 cells in the presence of the potent P-gp inhibitor, GF120918
[Report CD2008/00421]. The passive membrane permeability of [14C]daprodustat (3µM) was
determined at pH 7.4, and the absorptive membrane permeability of [14C]daprodustat (3µM) was
determined at pH 5.5 and 7.4 to simulate conditions in the gastro-intestinal tract. Permeabilities
were measured using MDCKII-hMDR1 cell monolayers in the presence of GF120918.
[14C]daprodustat was determined to have moderate passive membrane permeability of 21 nm/s at
pH 7.4. The absorptive membrane permeabilities of [14C]daprodustat in the presence of FaSSIF
were both high at pH 7.4 and pH 5.5 with a P7.4[abs] value of 191 nm/s and a P5.5[abs] value of
325 nm/s. The presence of biorelevant buffer, FaSSIF, increased the permeability of daprodustat.
In addition, daprodustat permeability is increased at pH 5.5 (Table 183).

P-gp Transport Inhibition
Daprodustat was evaluated as an in vitro inhibitor of human P-gp [Report CD2008/00669].
Inhibition of P-gp-mediated transport of digoxin (0.03µM) by daprodustat (0.1 to 100µM) was
investigated by determining the basolateral to apical ([B→A]) transport of [3H]digoxin by
MDCKII-hMDR1 cells transfected with the human MDR1 gene, which express the P-gp protein.
Daprodustat did not inhibit transport of digoxin via human P-gp.
Evaluation as Human BCRP Substrate
The bi-directional transport of daprodustat (3µM) was investigated using MDCKII-breast cancer
resistance protein (BCRP) cells transfected with the human BCRP gene and heterologously

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

OATP1B1 and OAT1B3 Substrate Potential
To evaluate the substrate potential of daprodustat and its six predominant circulating human
metabolites; M2 (GSK2391220), M3 (GSK2506104), M4 (GSK2487818), M5 (GSK2506102),
M6 (GSK2531398) and M13 (GSK2531401), the time-dependent transport of daprodustat and
metabolites (0.01 to 10µM) in OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 transporter expressing Human
Embryonic Kidney (HEK-293) cells was measured by LC-MS/MS [Report 2015N255319].

substrate of OATP1B1 but was a substrate of OATP1B3. M2, M4, M5, and M6 were substrates
of the OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 transporters. M3 was not a substrate of OATP1B1 nor
OATP1B3.
Inhibition of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3
In a study to determine if daprodustat is an inhibitor of the human OATP1B1 and OATP1B3, the
inhibition of uptake of the probe substrate [3H]estradiol 17β-D-glucuronide ([3H]EG) by
daprodustat (0.1 to 100µM, n=3/concentration) was investigated using OATP1B1
(heterologously expressed in Chinese Hamster Ovary (CHO) cells) and OATP1B3 (expressed in
human embryonic kidney MSRII (HEK-MSRII) cells after transduction of a BacMam
baculovirus coding for OATP1B3) [Report CD2008/00231]. Cells were incubated for 5 minutes
for OATP1B1 and 10 minutes for OATP1B3. For analysis of total radioactivity, the contents of
each well were determined by liquid scintillation counting in glass vials containing scintillant.
Daprodustat inhibited OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 with calculated IC50 values of 6µM and 11µM,
respectively.

Daprodustat Uptake by Human Hepatocytes

2015N232657]. Incubations performed in the presence of inhibitor cocktail were used to
determine the passive uptake of daprodustat into the human hepatocytes. The inhibitor cocktail
included the rifamycin SV and cyclosporine A (inhibitors of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3),
montelukast (inhibitor of OATP2B1) and imipramine (inhibitor of OCT1). [3H]estradiol
glucuronide ([3H]EG) (0.02µM) was used as positive control substrate in a separate set of
incubations under the conditions stated. Daprodustat uptake in cryopreserved human hepatocytes
from 3 different donors, at 0.5 or 0.3µM, was not inhibited by the inhibitor cocktail, suggesting a
primarily passive process is involved. Uptake of [3H]EG was inhibited by the inhibitor cocktail,
with a signal-to-noise ratio between 3.4 and 7.9, demonstrating functionality of the cell system.
There was no notable difference (≥2-fold) in daprodustat transport into cryopreserved human
hepatocytes in the presence of prototypical inhibitors (cyclosporine A, rifamycin, montelukast,
and imipramine) of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OATP2B1 or OCT1 transporters, suggesting that
daprodustat is not a substrate of these transporters.
M4 Uptake by Human Hepatocytes
In a study to investigate the time dependent, active uptake in the in vitro hepatic uptake of
daprodustat metabolite M4 (GSK2487818) (0.2, 2 and 15µM) using pooled cryopreserved
human hepatocytes, uptake experiments were conducted in the presence and absence of an
inhibitor cocktail [Report 2015N259558]. Incubations performed in the presence of inhibitor
cocktail were used to determine the passive uptake of M4 into the human hepatocytes. The
inhibitor cocktail included the rifamycin SV and imipramine. [3H]estradiol glucuronide
([3H]EG) (0.02µM) was used as positive control substrate in a separate set of incubations under
the conditions stated. M4 uptake in cryopreserved pooled human hepatocytes at 15µM was not
inhibited by the inhibitor cocktail, suggesting a primarily passive process is involved. Uptake of
[3H]EG was inhibited by the inhibitor cocktail, with a signal to noise ratio of 3.8 to 4.2,
demonstrating functionality of the cell system. There was no notable difference (≥2-fold) in M4
transport into pooled cryopreserved human hepatocytes in the presence of prototypical inhibitors
(rifamycin, and imipramine) of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OATP2B1 or OCT1 transporters,
suggesting that M4 is not a substrate of these transporters in a qualified physiologically relevant
system.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
Single Dose in Renal Impairment Subjects (Study PHI112843)
Title: A Phase I, single-dose, randomized and single-blind (Part 1) fixed sequence and open-label
(Part 2), study to evaluate the safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of
GSK1278863A in subjects with renal impairment and matched healthy volunteers.
Study Design: This was a single-center, Phase I, 2-part study. Part 1 was conducted in subjects
with moderate (Stage 3; eGFR 30 to 59 mL/min/1.73 m2) to severe (Stage 4; eGFR 15 to
29 mL/min/1.73 m2) renal impairment (Cohort 1) and subjects with normal renal function (eGFR
≥90 mL/min/1.73 m2; Cohort 2) and employed a single-dose, 2-period crossover, randomized
sequence, with daprodustat dose levels of 50 and 150 mg. Part 2 was conducted in subjects with
hemodialysis-dependent renal impairment (Stage 5; eGFR <15 mL/min/1.73 m2; Cohort 3) and
employed a single-dose, 2-period, fixed sequence, open-label design. During Part 2, study drug
was administered before dialysis in Period 1 and post-dialysis in Period 2, with a single
daprodustat dose of 150 mg in both periods. Blood samples from the arterial and venous side of
the circulation were compared at fixed timepoints following dosing during an active dialysis
session (Period 1) to evaluate the effect of dialysis on daprodustat plasma concentrations.
Treatment periods for both Part 1 and Part 2 were separated by a 7 to 10-day washout period.
Biomarkers associated with erythropoiesis, namely EPO, reticulocyte count, hemoglobin (Hb),
Hct, RBC, and hepcidin as well as markers related iron metabolism and utilization, including
serum ferritin, TIBC, and UIBC, were also assessed. VEGF was also measured in this study.
Pharmacodynamic (PD) assessments occurred out to 48 hours post-dose.
Results and Conclusions
Pharmacokinetics
Daprodustat pharmacokinetic (PK) parameters in subjects with Stages 3 to 4 (moderate to
severe) renal impairment and their matched healthy subjects are similar. Daprodustat exposure
[AUC(0-inf)] increased in a dose-proportional manner for the 50 and 150 mg doses. There were
no marked differences in daprodustat PK between subjects with moderate to severe renal
impairment (Cohort 1) and matched healthy subjects (Cohort 2). Daprodustat PK was similar in
hemodialysis subjects (Cohort 3) on a non-dialysis day and subjects with moderate to severe
renal impairment (Cohort 1). In HDD subjects (Cohort 3), plasma daprodustat exposures tended to
be higher during an active dialysis session compared to those observed on the day without dialysis,
indicating a reduction in the clearance of daprodustat during the dialysis procedure. There were no
consistent differences in daprodustat concentrations between the arterial and venous samples,
suggesting daprodustat was not significantly cleared by hemodialysis.

Repeat Dose (14 Days) in Renal Impairment Subjects (Study PHI115573)
Study Title: A repeat-dose, open-label, parallel-group study to assess the pharmacokinetics of
daprodustat and metabolites in normal subjects and subjects with impaired renal function.
Study Design: This was a repeat-dose, parallel-group study in subjects with normal renal
function, non-hemodialysis subjects with renal impairment (Stages 3/4/5 CKD), and
hemodialysis subjects with Stage 5 CKD. All subjects received a 5 mg daprodustat dose once
daily for 14 days (normal renal function and non-hemodialysis subjects) or 15 days
(hemodialysis subjects). Blood samples were collected on Days 1 and 14 for all subjects and
additionally on Day 15 for hemodialysis subjects to allow for collection on dialysis and nondialysis days. EPO and hepcidin were also assessed.
Results and Conclusions
Pharmacokinetics
Daprodustat exposure (AUC0-∞ and Cmax) following single and/or repeat doses was not
clinically different in subjects with normal renal function (190 ng.h/mL and 93 ng/mL,
respectively), subjects with Stage 3/4 non-dialysis (ND) CKD (176 ng.h/mL and 75 ng/mL),
or subjects with Stage 5 hemodialysis CKD on a dialysis day [213 ng.h/mL and 74 ng/mL]
and on a non-dialysis day [188 ng.h/mL and 76 ng/mL]. The steady-state Cmax of daprodustat
was 18 to 21% lower in all subjects with renal impairment. Overall, the PK of daprodustat
was not meaningfully affected by hemodialysis.
The major circulating metabolites, defined as ≥10% drug-related material (DRM; equal to
AUC of metabolite divided by the sum of AUC of all metabolites and parent) in plasma, were
M2, M3, and M13 in all populations assessed.
Daprodustat was not substantially eliminated by the kidney or dialysis, while elimination of
its metabolites, being more polar than parent drug, was more susceptible to changes in renal
function and dialysis. Dialysis clearance (CLd) values for daprodustat were 7.61 and
7.00 mL/min at 3 and 4 h post-dose, respectively, with negligible extraction ratios, while
metabolite CLd values at 3 and 4 h post-dose ranged from 140 to 167 mL/min with extraction
ratios >0.42.
Daprodustat did not accumulate in subjects with normal renal function, subjects with Stage
3/4 ND, or hemodialysis Stage 5 CKD subjects. At steady state, daprodustat was highly
bound to plasma protein (percent protein bound >99%) in all subject groups. The plasma
protein binding of M4 and M5 was lower (~31 to 41% at the 3-hour time point) in subjects

with renal impairment compared to healthy subjects (~45 and 55%). This may be due to loss
of protein in urine, which is common with CKD.
For subjects with Stage 3/4 ND and Stage 5 hemodialysis CKD (dialysis day), exposure
parameter values (AUC and Cmax) of all daprodustat predominant metabolites were higher than in
subjects with normal renal function (Table 187). The exception to this was for metabolites M4,
M6 and M13 where a slightly lower Cmax was observed for subjects with Stage 5 hemodialysis
CKD (dialysis day). Subjects with Stage 5 hemodialysis CKD on a non-dialysis day had the
highest metabolite exposure. Lower AUC and Cmax values for the predominant metabolites were
observed on a dialysis day compared to a non-dialysis day; however, the apparent t½ were
comparable between the 2 cohorts (except for metabolite M13, which was longer on a dialysis
day), indicative of dialysis clearance of metabolites.
For subjects with Stage 3/4 ND and Stage 5 hemodialysis CKD (dialysis day), the higher
exposure and reduced elimination of metabolites results in accumulation for most metabolites
(accumulation ratios [Rac] up to 1.4-fold in subjects with Stage 3/4 ND; for Stage 5 CKD,
accumulation ratios were calculated based on half-life estimates to be up to 1.8-fold and 1.4-fold
for dialysis day and non-dialysis day, respectively, except for M13, for which Rac values were
2.6-fold and 1.5-fold, respectively). No apparent time dependencies from single-dose to
multiple-dose administration were observed for any predominant metabolite.

Pharmacodynamics
In Stage 3/4 ND CKD subjects and Stage 5 hemodialysis CKD subjects, mean EPO
concentrations increased from baseline and reached a maximum at 8 to 12 h post-dose
and returned approximately to baseline levels at 24 h post-dose. In Stage 3/4 CKD
subjects, the maximum observed increase in EPO on Day 14 occurred at 8 hours postdose and was ~2.4-fold the pre-dose value on Day 14. In Stage 5 CKD subjects, the
maximum observed increases on Days 14 and 15 also occurred at 8 hours post-dose and
were ~4.7-fold (Day 14) and ~6.2-fold (Day 15) the pre-dose value on those days,
respectively. There was no evidence of EPO accumulation in either population.
Repeat Dose (14 days) in End-Stage Renal Disease Subjects on Peritoneal Dialysis (Study
200942)
Study Title: A repeat-dose, open-label study to assess the pharmacokinetics of daprodustat
and metabolites in subjects with End-Stage Renal Disease undergoing peritoneal dialysis.
277
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

Study Design: This was a repeat dose, single arm study in End-Stage Renal Disease (ESRD)
subjects undergoing peritoneal dialysis. Subjects received oral daprodustat at a dose of 5 mg
once per day for 14 days. Serial blood samples were collected on Days 1 and 14 for PK and
PD assessments, with additional pre-dose samples collected during the study. Dialysate PK
samples were collected on Days 1, 14, and 15. Assessed PD markers included EPO and
hepcidin.
Daprodustat was readily absorbed with a median time to maximum concentration (Tmax) at 1- and
2-hours post-dose following single and repeat daprodustat doses, respectively. Daprodustat was
rapidly eliminated from systemic circulation in ESRD subjects undergoing peritoneal dialysis
with a t½ of approximately 2 hours following single and repeat doses. Daprodustat did not
accumulate in plasma (geometric least squares [GLS] mean ratio: 0.896) and exhibited timeindependent PK following repeat daprodustat doses in ESRD subjects.
Daprodustat metabolites appeared slowly in plasma with the median tmax ranging from 3 to
9 hours post-dose, and the t½ of daprodustat metabolites ranged from 3 to 27 hours following
repeat daprodustat doses. All daprodustat metabolites were at steady-state concentrations
following 14 days of oral daprodustat doses in ESRD subjects. Following 14 days of
daprodustat doses, all the daprodustat metabolites appeared to accumulate in plasma, except
for M4 (GLS mean ratio range: 1.02 [M4] to 1.95 [M13]). M2, M3, and M13 were the major
circulating metabolites of daprodustat in the ESRD subjects, as defined by >10% DRM in
plasma. The results of the study showed no changes in protein binding at pre-dose, 3 hours,
and 12 hours post-dosing for daprodustat, M4, and M5. Daprodustat was >99% protein
bound, and the 2 metabolites measured (M4 and M5) were approximately 69 to 76% protein
bound in subjects undergoing peritoneal dialysis.

Pharmacodynamics
Baseline EPO concentrations were similar between continuous ambulatory peritoneal
dialysis (CAPD; 13.9 IU/L) and automated peritoneal dialysis (APD; 17.0 IU/L) subjects.
EPO concentrations increased post-dose, reaching maximum levels at 8 to 12 hours postdose, and then declined to pre-dose values by 24 hours. Maximal concentrations were 32.2
and 44.8 IU/L for the CAPD and APD subjects, respectively. For the ESRD subjects, EPO
levels tended to be lower on Day 14 as compared to Day 1, and high variability in EPO
concentrations was observed. Similar patterns were observed for CAPD and APD subjects.
Food Effect in Healthy Subjects and Drug Interaction With a Strong CYP2C8 Inhibitor
Gemfibrozil (Study PHI113634)
Study Title: Open-label study to assess the pharmacokinetics of daprodustat co-administered with
a high-fat meal or an inhibitor of CYP2C8 (gemfibrozil).
Study Design: This was a single-center, open-label, single-sequence study in healthy subjects to
assess the PK of oral daprodustat administered alone or co-administered with gemfibrozil (strong
CYP2C8 inhibitor; oral). Subjects were administered 3 treatments: daprodustat 100 mg (fasted),
daprodustat 100 mg (high-fat meal), and daprodustat 100 mg plus gemfibrozil 600 mg at steady
state.
Table 189. Study Design for PHI113634

Source: Table 7 Summary of clinical pharmacology
Abbreviations: BID, twice daily

Results and Conclusions
Twenty-three subjects were enrolled, and 21 subjects completed the study. Co-administration of
a single 100 mg daprodustat dose with a high-fat, high-calorie meal resulted in an 11 and 31%
decrease in daprodustat AUC(0-inf) and Cmax, respectively, and a 1 h mean delay of tmax relative to
fasted administration. Similar t½ values were observed for the two prandial states. The 90%
confidence interval (CI) of the adjusted geometric mean ratio was 0.84-1.01 for AUC(0-inf) and
0.61-0.82 for Cmax, concluding that a high-fat meal did not have a clinically significant effect on
the AUC and Cmax of daprodustat. For daprodustat metabolites, slight increases in exposure
(AUC(0-inf)) were observed when daprodustat was co-administered with food. The 90% CIs of
AUC(0-inf) GLSM ratios for daprodustat metabolites ranged from 0.99 to 1.21.

Co-administration of a single 100 mg dose of daprodustat with a strong CYP2C8 inhibitor
(gemfibrozil) resulted in a 3.9- and 18.6-fold increase in daprodustat Cmax and AUC(0-inf),
respectively. This significant increase in both rate and extent of systemic exposure of daprodustat
when co-administered with gemfibrozil is consistent with daprodustat being primarily
metabolized by CYP2C8. The decrease in CYP2C8 metabolism of daprodustat is reflected by its
~4-fold increase in t½, 2.51 h to 9.26 h when co-administered with gemfibrozil. Tmax was also
delayed from 1.00 to 4.00 h. The appearance of daprodustat metabolites was delayed by ~9-10 h
as tmax shifted from 3-4 h for each metabolite to ~12 h when daprodustat was co-administered
with gemfibrozil; the Cmax and AUC(0-inf) of metabolites were reduced by at least 90% and 67%,
respectively.

Food Effect (CKD Meal) in Healthy Subjects (Study 207727 Part 2)
Study Title: A single center, single dose, open-label, randomized, 2-way crossover study in
healthy Japanese male subjects to evaluate the bioequivalence of daprodustat tablets (2 mg tablet
vs. 4 mg tablet) (Part 1) and the food effect on the pharmacokinetics of daprodustat (Part 2).
Study Design: The purpose of Part 2 of this study was to assess the effect of food on the singledose PK of daprodustat in healthy Japanese male subjects administered the commercial tablet
formulation.
Part 2 was a single-center, single dose, fed/fasted, open-label, randomized, 2-way crossover
study in healthy Japanese male subjects. Subjects received a daprodustat 4 mg tablet
(commercial formulation made by Process 1) 30 minutes after the end of a standard CKD meal
(500-700 kcal, comprised of 12-16 g protein, 1 to 2 g salt, and up to 500 mg potassium) or in the
fasted state. Following a washout period of 5 days, subjects crossed over to receive treatment in
the opposite prandial state. Blood samples for PK analysis were collected prior to dosing and
until 24 hours post-dose following each dose.
Results and Conclusions: Twelve subjects enrolled in Part 2 (food effect) of this study and all
subjects completed the study. Following administration of daprodustat under fed and fasted
conditions, similar daprodustat peak concentrations were observed though Tmax was reached
1 hour later in the fed state. There were no apparent differences in t½ between fed and fasted
states. Metabolites were not analyzed in this study.

Single Ascending Dose Study in Healthy Adults (Study PHX111427)
Study Title: A Phase I, randomized, single-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-escalation study to
evaluate the safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of single oral doses of
GSK1278863A in healthy subjects.
Study Design: Study PHX111427 was a single-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, doserising, single dose, sequential parallel group study to investigate the safety, tolerability, PK and
PD of GSK1278863A in healthy adult subjects. Subjects were dosed sequentially in six
ascending dosing cohorts corresponding to six dose levels ranging from 2 mg to 300 mg of
GSK1278863A. Within each cohort, a total of six subjects were randomized to receive a single,
oral dose of daprodustat or placebo in a ratio of 2:1 (four active: two placebo). Thirty-six
subjects were enrolled and completed the study.
Results and Conclusion
Daprodustat was readily absorbed following single dose administration with the median
maximum concentration achieved between 1.25-2.00 hours post dose across different dose
levels. Daprodustat was eliminated from systemic circulation rapidly with short terminal halflives ranging from 0.8-4.0 hours across the dose range of 2 mg-300 mg.

Multiple Ascending Dose Study in Healthy Adults (Study PHI112842)
Study Title: A Phase I, randomized, single-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-escalation study to
evaluate the safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of repeat oral doses of
GSK1278863A in healthy subjects.

283
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

Study Design: Subjects were dosed in 5 dosing cohorts corresponding to 4 once-daily regimens
(15 to 100 mg) and one twice daily (BID) regimen (25 mg in the morning and evening). Dosing
cohorts consisted of six subjects. Within each cohort, subjects were randomly assigned to receive
either GSK1278863A or placebo in a 2:1 ratio (4 active:2 placebo). Each subject was dosed daily
for up to 14 days.
Results and Conclusion
A total of 30 subjects were randomized to treatment (Figure 48). Of these, 29 completed the
study, and one subject in Regimen D (25 mg BID) discontinued due to an adverse event.
Daprodustat was absorbed with a median Tmax range 1 to 4 hours and was eliminated from
systemic circulation with t½ values ranging from 1.0 to 2.3 h. Daprodustat exposure (Cmax, AUC)
increased dose proportionally following daily doses up to 100 mg. Daprodustat did not
significantly accumulate in plasma over 14 days of dosing, and steady-state plasma
concentrations were achieved by Day 2.

The Applicant claimed that a 3-compartment PopPK model with first-order elimination from the
central compartment together with 5 absorption transit compartments, best described daprodustat
PK. Allometric scaling was applied to all drug clearance (CL) and volume terms using fixed
exponents (0.75 and 1.00 respectively) to account for PK variability related to body weight.
The final daprodustat PK model parameter estimates and the corresponding 95% confidence
intervals (CIs) from Bootstrap are presented in Table 200. The goodness-of-fit plots for the final
daprodustat PK model are presented in Figure 49. The analysis plan included the Goodness-ofFit (GoF) plots that use the normalized predicted distribution error (NPDE) instead of
conditional weighted residuals (CWRES), as CWRES is not defined for BLQ observations,
NPDE take the likelihood of the BLQ data into account and are, therefore, more appropriate for
the M3 method that was used to model the PK data. Due to the use of the M3 method to
incorporate the BLQ data, the prediction-corrected visual predictive check (VPC) could not be
generated. The VPC based on all data from five studies (Figure 50) illustrated the prediction
percentiles and corresponding 95% CI of simulated concentrations overlaid on the observed
daprodustat concentrations and the corresponding 5th and 95th percentiles.

Covariate analysis showed only two significant covariates were identified: concomitant
clopidogrel use reduced daprodustat clearance by 42.4%, and subjects that were non-dialysis
dependent had a 45.6% higher ka compared to dialysis dependent subjects. There are no
significant covariate effects of age, body weight, sex, or hyporesponder status. Body weight, as a
covariate, was implemented in the PopPK model as allometric exponents applied to CL and V
model parameters. The simulated covariate effects on Cmax and AUC from body weight, dialysis
and Study 205665, and concomitant drug use relative to a reference subject (non-dialysis
dependent, not using clopidogrel, body weight of 74.6 kg) are shown in Figure 51, Figure 52,
and Table 201. Body weight, as a covariate, was implemented in the PopPK model as allometric
exponents applied to CL and V model parameters. The AUC ratio for subjects weighing >75 kg
to < 75 kg (estimate: 0.74, 90% CI: 0.68 – 0.80) and corresponding Cmax ratio (estimate 0.68,
90% CI: 0.64 – 0.73) suggests these changes are unlikely to be clinically relevant given the
individualized treatment modality of daprodustat in CKD.
Of note, the maximum daprodustat dose level in the current analysis dataset was 24 mg for the
QD dose studies and 48 mg for the TIW dose study 204837. Additional simulations were
performed with the final POPK model to calculate the exposure metrics for a typical dialysis
patient (without concomitant clopidogrel use and 74.6 kg body weight) at the maximum
daprodustat dose level and is shown in Table 202.

Reviewer’s Comments
The Applicant’s final population PK model as described above is adequate to describe the
daprodustat serum concentration-time profiles of patients with CKD in studies 205665, 204837,
201410, 200807 and 20080, following the administration of daprodustat ranging from 1 mg to
48 mg QD. The shrinkage value was 14.6% for IIV on CL/F, 7.6% for IIV on ka, and condition
number was 12.8, indicating there was no obvious bias in the parameter estimates. Therefore, the
PK model is acceptable for simulating post-hoc exposure metrics, e.g., Cmax and Ctau of
daprodustat for exposure-response (E-R) analyses.
Covariate analysis identified 2 statistically significant covariates: concomitant clopidogrel use
reduced clearance by 42.4%, and subjects that were non-dialysis dependent had 45.6% higher ka
compared to dialysis dependent subjects. There are no significant covariate effects of age, body
weight, sex, or hyporesponder status. The impact of the following intrinsic and extrinsic factors
on the population pharmacokinetics of daprodustat in patients with CKD were quantified using
simulations of typical subjects with the final model: dialysis (no change in AUC and -14% in
Cmax), and concomitant clopidogrel use (+74% AUC and +18% Cmax).

305
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

However, a PopPK-based DDI evaluation for concomitant clopidogrel use can be limited due to
the following reasons: inadequacy of sample size, lack of clear medication history prior to study
entry, lack of dosing records for concomitant medications (e.g., dose level, dose frequency,
formulation, route of administration, start and stop date and clock time, duration, dose
modification, presence of other concomitant medications were not recorded), missing food,
and/or other potential confounding factors, and inadequacy of PK sampling timepoints relative to
concomitant medication dosing. In addition, the Applicant only used a binary covariate for
concomitant clopidogrel use rather than a time varying covariate model.
In summary, the Applicant’s current PopPK based DDI assessment is not adequate, which may
significantly underestimate the true DDI effect due to the following reasons:
1. The binary covariate analysis was performed in the Applicant’s PopPK modeling without
clear dosing history.
2. Lack of adequate daprodustat PK samples during the elimination phase in clinical trials.
3. Lack of medication history prior to study entry (that may have prolonged effects on drug
absorption, distribution, metabolism, and elimination) and dosing records for concomitant
medications: e.g., dose level, dose frequency, formulation, route of administration, start
and stop date and clock time, duration, dose modification, in PopPK dataset.
The Applicant’s justification based on efficacy/safety for minimum PK effect of clopidogrel and
phosphate binders, iron, or acid reducing agents coadministration (use/no use) are also not
acceptable. These can be confounded by dose titration to maintain target Hb range of 10 to
11.5 g/dL. Therefore, the Applicant’s current PopPK based DDI assessments are not adequate.
To support TIW dosing for dialysis patients, PopPK simulations were performed. The results
showed the weekly AUC is slightly higher with 24 mg QD compared to 48 mg TIW,
corresponding to the higher weekly dose in the former (168 mg) compared to the latter (144 mg).
In summary, the PopPK model supports no dose adjustment for the above-mentioned specific
populations such as age, gender, body weight, and non-dialysis dependent status but is not
sensitive to evaluate drug interactions.

14.5.2. Summary of Applicant’s Population Dose
response model
The initial daprodustat dose response analysis of Hb in anemic subjects with chronic kidney
disease was developed in 2015 using 11,810 Hb observations in a total of 710 subjects. The
structural model used to describe the time course of Hb was comprised of a precursor cell
compartment, where the baseline production of precursors was a function of baseline Hb, and 12
transit compartments that described the RBC lifespan as shown in Figure 53.

Reviewer’s Comments
The Applicant’s final population dose Hb model developed in 2015 as described above is
adequate to describe the Hb concentration time profiles of patients with CKD after treatment
with placebo or daprodustat (0.5 to 25 mg QD). Model diagnostics and predictive performance of
the model (as assessed by prediction and variability corrected predictive checks) were
acceptable. Based on model simulations, the proposed starting doses, dose levels, and dose
adjustment scheme appear to be appropriate and were predicted to lead to Hb levels within the
target range of 10 to 11 g/dL, which was subsequently confirmed in phase 3 clinical trials.

14.5.3. Summary of Applicant’s Exposure-Response
Analysis
14.5.3.1. Longitudinal Exposure-Response Analysis to
Quantify the Effect of Daprodustat on Hemoglobin
Changes in Subjects With Chronic Kidney Disease
Undergoing Dialysis From Studies 200807, 204837
and 204836
(b) (4)

TIW dosing regimen versus QD in subjects with CKD undergoing
dialysis, multivariate longitudinal population exposure-response analysis was conducted using
three studies: 200807, 204837 and 204836. The initial daprodustat dose response Hb model in
anemic subjects with CKD was developed in 2015 to support phase 3 dose selection (see details
in section 14.5.2). The Applicant further updated their 2019 Dose-Hb model with longitudinal
Hb data from study 204836 and data from three Japanese Phase 3 studies. Lastly, additional Hb
data from 204837 and 200807 studies were used to further update their final 2022 Dose-Hb
model. The above dose response models showed consistent parameters estimates and model
predictive performance.
For the current longitudinal population concentration-Hb (Conc-Hb) model, the PK data from
subgroup populations from the three studies (200807, 204837 and 204836) with corresponding
Hb levels were pooled and analyzed using a population approach with evaluation of patient
covariates on the model parameters.

PK and Hb profiles over time were simulated using the final PK-PD model for a typical subject

(thromboembolic event, nonfatal myocardial infarction (MI), and nonfatal hospitalization for

For each PK Population subject in these two studies, the average cumulative dose (i.e., total dose
for each subject divided by duration of dosing up to the time of the safety event or 52 weeks in
those without a safety event) was calculated to obtain the average QD or TIW dose and the
observed Cmax from the corresponding PK Day dose was extrapolated to determine the Cmax at
the subject’s estimated average QD or TIW dose. The extrapolated average Cmax up to the time of
the event was calculated as the observed Cmax divided by the dose administered on the PK Day
multiplied by average QD or TIW dose. Results are summarized in Table 208. For all safety
endpoints, there was a small number of events. For MI and thromboembolic events, the
confidence intervals for the hazard ratio (HR) and the odd ratio (OR) included one.

317
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

Following oral administration, daprodustat is readily absorbed with median Tmax in healthy
subjects ranging from 1 to 4 hours. Daprodustat exhibited linear PK with generally doseproportional increases in plasma exposure from 2 mg to 300 mg. The absolute bioavailability of
daprodustat is 65%. Daprodustat has an approximately equal distribution between plasma and
blood cells. Following intravenous dosing, the volume of distribution at steady state in healthy
subjects is 14.3 L (Study 200232). Following oral or intravenous administration of radiolabeled
daprodustat to healthy adults, approximately 40% of the total circulating radioactivity in plasma
was daprodustat, and the remaining 60% was metabolites. Three metabolites each accounting for
more than 10% of circulating drug-related material have been identified (Study 200942). Within
seven days of an oral dose of radiolabeled daprodustat, 74% of the radioactivity was recovered in
the feces, and 21% in the urine. Approximately 99.5% of the dose was excreted as oxidative
metabolites, with the rest accounted for by daprodustat (Study 200232).
Daprodustat clearance was driven by cytochrome P450 (CYP) 2C8 metabolism (in vitro
fmCYP2C8 of ~96%, CD2010/00300/00) with a minor contribution by CYP3A4 metabolism and
urinary excretion (2012N157420_01). Gemfibrozil, a strong CYP2C8 inhibitor, increased
daprodustat AUC and Cmax by 19- and 3.9-fold, respectively (Study PHI113634). Trimethoprim,
a weak CYP2C8 inhibitor, increased AUC and Cmax of daprodustat by 1.5 and 1.3-fold,
respectively (Study 200229).
In vitro, daprodustat is not a competitive or time-dependent inhibitor of CYPs 1A2, 2A6, 2B6,
2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4. Daprodustat inhibited CYP2C8 with a calculated half-maximal
inhibitory concentration IC50 value of 21µM (study UH2008, CD2008). Daprodustat is not an in
vitro inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 (study CD2008). Daprodustat is a BCRP
substrate in vitro but not a substrate of human drug transporters P-gp, OATP1B1, OATP1B3,
OATP2B1 OCT1, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1, or MATE2-K. Daprodustat is not an inhibitor
of transporters P-gp, BCRP, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2-K in vitro. Daprodustat
is an in vitro inhibitor of human OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 (study UH2008, CD2008).
Methods
PBPK Software
Simcyp v20 (Certara, UK) was used by the Applicant to develop the daprodustat PBPK models
and predict the PK of daprodustat with and without CYP2C8 modulators. The reviewer used the
same software and version for analyses.
Model Development
During PBPK model development, the ADAM module in Simcyp was used to investigate the
mechanistic drug absorption and dissolution profiles. A minimal PBPK model was employed to
understand the tissue distribution of daprodustat. The final input parameters for daprodustat are
summarized in Table 209.

Population, Victim, and Perpetrator Drug Models
The Simcyp software built-in virtual healthy volunteer population and clinically relevant probes
substrates, inhibitors or inducers have been utilized. The default PBPK models of gemfibrozil
and trimethoprim in Simcyp were used for DDI prediction. The PBPK models of clopidogrel and
its glucuronide were developed based on published data (Tornio et al. 2014; Shebley et al. 2017;
Varma et al. 2019). Simcyp built-in models of repaglinide and the PBPK models of pioglitazone
and montelukast built by the Applicant based on were used to qualify the ability of the
clopidogrel PBPK model to simulate DDIs with CYP2C8 substrates as well as a CYP3A4
substrate. No modifications were made to the original models.

Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

PBPK Simulation Trial Design
Simulations for daprodustat PBPK qualification and verification used a fixed study population,
where individual subject data were available to match the observed clinical PK. Simulations
were designed to replicate the situation in the actual daprodustat clinical studies, with either the
exact number of subjects or sufficient subject numbers, sex, proportion of males and females in
appropriate virtual populations of healthy volunteer subjects in replicate N trials to have a
representative total of at least 100 subjects or more. Where individually matched patient
demographic data and PK information were not available (e.g., model application), simulations
were conducted using the default ranges within the Simcyp simulator (v20).
Model Refinement and Validation




The PBPK model was validated against the adult PK studies following a single
intravenous dose of 0.05 mg (study 200232), single oral doses of 2 mg, 5 mg, and 15 mg
(study PHX111427) and repeated (QD) oral doses of 5 mg, 15 mg, and 100 mg (studies
PHI112842, PHI116008 and PHI115573) daprodustat in healthy subjects.
The PBPK model was optimized and validated with DDI studies in adult healthy subjects
treated with gemfibrozil (study PHI113634) and trimethoprim (study 200229)
respectively.

Model Application
The daprodustat PBPK model was applied to perform prospective drug-drug risk assessment as a
victim after coadministration of clopidogrel
FDA’s Assessment




As stated in the PBPK report by the Applicant that ‘the clopidogrel PBPK base model
was developed in Simcyp based on published data (Tornio et al. 2014; Shebley et al.
2017; Varma et al. 2019). An information request was issued requesting the Applicant to
provide evidence that the cited models of clopidogrel and its glucuronide could simulate
the plasma concentration-time profiles and predict the magnitude of interaction observed
in drug interaction studies using Simcyp simulator version 20.
In the PBPK report, PBPK models of pioglitazone and montelukast used to qualify the
clopidogrel models are currently not available in Simcyp v20. An information request
was issued requesting the Applicant to submit the full documentation of model
development and model verification for pioglitazone and montelukast using the Simcyp
V20, including comparison of predicted and observed PK profiles, PK parameters and
DDIs with CYP inhibitors.

Applicant’s Response to FDA’s IR and FDA’s Assessment
In the response to our IR, the Applicant provided additional details and verification regarding the
clopidogrel and clopidogrel-glucuronide Simcyp v20 PBPK models used in PBPK report
(2019N396674). Briefly, the Applicant compared the simulated PK parameter values with those
observed clinical data extracted from a variety of clinical studies for both clopidogrel and
clopidogrel glucuronide. As shown in Table 210, the PK parameters of clopidogrel-glucuronide,

the major driving force for CYP2C8 inhibition, were significantly underpredicted compared with
the observed clinical data for several studies. These discrepancies might be potentially explained
by between-study variability; as shown in Figure 59, a majority of the observed concentrations
fell between the 5th and 95th of the prediction confidence intervals. The ability of the clopidogrel
models to predict their interactions with CYP2C8 substrates will be discussed in the Results
section.
The Applicant developed PBPK models of pioglitazone and montelukast based on references and
verified these models by comparing the simulated PK profiles and parameters with available
clinical literature reports after both IV and oral dosing routes. The results demonstrated that the
montelukast model could simulate the plasma PK profiles of montelukast reasonably well but the
pioglitazone model significantly overpredicted the clearance of pioglitazone following IV
administration (Table 211) and underpredicted pioglitazone AUC following oral administration
of multiple doses of 30 mg pioglitazone (Table 212). However, both montelukast and
pioglitazone models could predict the effects of gemfibrozil on their exposure (Table 213).

Results
Can Daprodustat PBPK Model Reasonably Describe the PK of Daprodustat?
Yes, PBPK simulations reasonably predicted the PK profiles of daprodustat following a single
intravenous dose of 0.05 mg, single oral doses of 2 mg, 5 mg, and 15 mg and multiple (QD) oral
doses of 5 mg, 15 mg, and 100 mg of daprodustat in healthy subjects, as demonstrated in Figure
60 and by a reasonable agreement between predicted and observed values for AUC and Cmax in
Table 214.

Can Daprodustat PBPK Model Reasonably Describe the Effects of Gemfibrozil and
Trimethoprim on the PK of Daprodustat in Healthy Subjects?
Yes, the contribution of the CYP2C8 pathway to daprodustat clearance (e.g., assumption of
fmCYP2C8) was verified and calibrated using clinical DDI data with gemfibrozil. Briefly, an in
vitro study using human liver microsomes attributed approximately 96% metabolism to
CYP2C8. Using this value as fm,CYP2C8 to determine recombinant CLint values resulted in
significant underprediction of the magnitude of DDI with gemfibrozil (predicted AUC ratio of
12-fold versus observed 18.6-fold). The Applicant optimized the fm,CYP2C8 value to 98.1% so that
the predicted AUC ratio (15.1-fold) was within 20% of the observed AUC ratio (Table 215). The
simulated and observed daprodustat plasma concentration-time profiles in the presence and
absence of gemfibrozil are shown in Figure 61. Notably, the increase in contribution via
CYP2C8 resulted in overprediction of DDI effect of trimethoprim (200 mg BID) on daprodustat
(Table 215). However, the optimized fm,CYP2C8 value is still acceptable, as it may give a
conservative estimate of the effect of clopidogrel on daprodustat DDI.

Can Daprodustat PBPK Model Predict the Effects of Clopidogrel on the PK of Daprodustat
Following Multiple Oral Dose Administration in Healthy Subjects?
The magnitude of interaction between clopidogrel and daprodustat may potentially be
underpredicted because the current clopidogrel PBPK model was not reliably validated. As
stated in the previous sections, the observed PK profiles of both clopidogrel and clopidogrelglucuronide could not be consistently reproduced by the PBPK models of clopidogrel and its
glucuronide (Table 210). Furthermore, these clopidogrel PBPK models significantly
underpredicted repaglinide DDI as indicated in Table 216, even though the predicted clopidogrel
and its glucuronide exposure was consistent with that observed in the study (Table 210) and the
repaglinide PBPK model was verified (data not shown). These findings suggest that these
clopidogrel PBPK models were not well validated and cannot be used confidently to predict the
DDI of daprodustat co-administered with clopidogrel.
328
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

To further evaluate the potential interaction between clopidogrel and daprodustat, the clinically
observed interactions of known CYP2C8 substrates with gemfibrozil or clopidogrel were
compared (Table 217). Based on the relative difference in the magnitude of interaction of
CYP2C8 substrates with clopidogrel and gemfibrozil, the PBPK model-predicted interaction
with clopidogrel following a single 300-mg clopidogrel approximated to the lower bound of the
values estimated from the clinical DDI data. Following steady state clopidogrel, there was less
data available to evaluate the model prediction. However, based on the results from the
interaction between repaglinide and clopidogrel, the interaction was expected to be similar to that
observed following a single 300-mg dose of clopidogrel. In addition, because daprodustat is the
most sensitive CYP2C8 substrate, increase in its AUC is expected to be greater compared to that
observed from repaglinide following the same clopidogrel treatment regimen. Therefore, there is
a potential that the interaction between daprodustat and clopidogrel following steady state
clopidogrel was underpredicted by the PBPK modeling approach. Taken together, the current
clopidogrel PBPK model was not reliably validated and the effects of clopidogrel on the PK of
daprodustat following multiple oral dose administration in healthy subjects may be
underpredicted.

Not applicable.

15. Study/Trial Design
15.1. Study 200808/ASCEND-ND
Protocol Amendments
There were 4 global amendments and 5 “country specific” local amendments to the original
protocol (dated June 2016). Protocol Amendment 05 (dated July 20, 2020) was used to prepare
the clinical study report (CSR) submitted with the application.
Amendment 01
Amendment 01 was implemented September 20, 2016 and applied to: Austria, Belgium, Czech
Republic, Denmark, Estonia, Germany, Hungary, Italy, Norway, Poland, Portugal, Romania,
Spain, Sweden and the United Kingdom. The main changes to the protocol included clarification
of the end of the study definition/criteria. The study rationale and inclusion criteria were revised.
Additional guidance was added to the iron management criteria, and a new exploratory objective
to compare the effect of daprodustat to rhEPO on delayed graft function (DGF) after deceased
donor kidney transplantation was added.
330
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

Amendment 02
Amendment 02 was implemented October 12, 2016 and applied to all countries and sites. It also
included country-specific requirements for France and Czech Republic. The main changes to the
protocol included the changes from country-specific Amendment 1. The section relating to
darbepoetin alfa pre-filled syringe incidents, malfunctions and user errors was deleted. The
Schedules of Assessments were modified to include new timepoints and prompts. The negatively
adjudicated events reporting process was updated. Historical kidney ultrasound and a provision
for a more sensitive imaging study (e.g., MRI, CT) to assess entry criteria were added.
Amendment 03
Amendment 03 was implemented October 5, 2017 and applied to all countries and sites. The
main changes to the protocol included a provision for possible adjustment to the Dose
Adjustment Algorithm triggers for Hb values 7.5 g/dL to <9.5 g/dL based on the review of
blinded instream aggregate Hb data. Retest values for Hb and transferrin saturation (TSAT) to
determine eligibility at Week -8 were added. Exclusion criteria were broadened. A provision was
added for unexpected circumstances where the supply to the site was interrupted. New
darbepoetin alfa dose strengths (not available in all countries) were added. Directions regarding
randomized treatment and study continuation for participants away from the research site for an
extended period of time were added.
Clarifications were made to the timeframe for iron management criteria. Visit windows were
revised and clarified. The FSH level to confirm menopause was updated.
The Schedules of Assessments were modified for clarification and to include new activities and
prompts. The additional inclusion and eligible visits to collect PK samples in PK sub-studies
were clarified. The patient reported outcome (PRO) section was updated to add healthcare
resource utilization data.
The statistical section was revised to change from two-sided testing at the 5% level to one-sided
testing at the 2.5% level; to clarify progression of CKD endpoint population and analysis model
and for secondary endpoints; to change significance levels to p-values; to correct the timepoint
for various PROs; to add a more complete description of the adjustments to statistical model; and
to add additional text regarding the interim analysis process. Exploratory endpoints around Hb
variability, iron parameters, transfusions, and dose adjustment scheme were added.
Adjustments to the objectives, endpoints and analysis of the ABPM sub-study were made.
Amendment 04
Amendment 04 was implemented August 16, 2019 and applied to all countries and sites. The
protocol was modified to add retest values for Hb at Day 1, an additional opportunity for TSAT
to determine eligibility at Week -8, and revised the definition of current uncontrolled
hypertension in order to determine eligibility. Hypothetical risk information, new required
assessments, and randomized treatment stopping criteria for participants with ADPKD were
added.
Worsening of hypertension was added as an adverse event of special interest.
Renal progression via change in eGFR was added as a secondary objective/endpoint.
331
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

For participants transitioning to dialysis, clarified the last historical clearance (Kt/Vurea)
measurement obtained per standard of care can be used. Stated recruitment in the ABPM substudy was closed in Amendment 4. Entry criteria to PK sub-study was revised; if the sub-study
recruitment did not make its pre-specified target, depending on the number of participants with
data, a limited number of analyses and data summaries may be produced.
Amendment 05
Amendment 05 was implemented July 30, 2020 and applied to all countries and sites. The
protocol was modified to revise the major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) non-inferiority
margin; as a result, the target MACE was also revised (to 664 events). The primary rationale for
these changes was to accelerate the closeout of the study in consideration of the COVID-19
pandemic and its impact on study integrity and data quality. Additionally, this change was
aligned with similar studies that had already compared the effects of HIF-PHIs on MACE versus
rhEPO, based on publicly available information. Analysis of the Hb co-primary endpoint and the
multiplicity adjustment strategy were updated. Pregnancy reporting timelines were updated to
align with revised Sponsor timings.
It should be noted that because the adjudication process for MACE may have taken time, some
MACE related analyses contain more than 664 events.
Protocol Deviations
Significant protocol deviations were reported for 66% of subjects in the daprodustat arm and
68% of subjects in the darbepoetin alfa arm. The majority of protocol deviations were due to
study procedures/assessments (44%) of total population (N=3872) and were balanced between
arms.
In Study 200808 (ASCEND-ND), data on 17 study participants were excluded from all analyses
as a valid informed consent was never obtained. During the conduct of the study, three sites
(223710, 228130, and 239601) were closed early due to good clinical practice (GCP) noncompliance issues including suspected fraud at one site. The site (23601) with suspected fraud
randomized one subject and the data from this site were included in the study displays. No
additional analyses were performed to exclude the participant’s data from this site. Data from
site 228130 were included in the study displays except for one screen-failed participant who did
not have a valid informed consent form. Data from site 223710 were not included in the data
displays due to invalid consent obtained from 14 participants at this stie.
Additional quality issues relating to GCP noncompliance (e.g., informed consent, dosing errors,
late reporting of serious adverse events (SAEs), unreported temperature excursions, central lab
issues, late reporting of individual case safety reports, missing research records) were identified
at other sites and corrective actions taken, and issues were reviewed for root causes and
addressed by Applicant.

15.2. Study 200807/ASCEND-D
Protocol Amendments
There were 3 global amendments and 4 “country-specific” local amendments to the original
protocol (dated June 9, 2016). Protocol Amendment 04 (dated July 30, 2020) was used to prepare
the CSR. A copy of Protocol Amendment 04 and each of the protocol amendments is found in
the modular appendix. A brief summary of each protocol amendment is provided below.
Amendment 01
Amendment 01 was implemented September 20, 2016 and applied to: Austria, Belgium, Czech
Republic, Denmark, Estonia, Germany, Hungary, Italy, Norway, Poland, Portugal, Romania,
Spain, Sweden and the United Kingdom. The main changes to the protocol included clarification
of the end of the study definition/criteria. The study rationale and inclusion criteria were revised.
Additional guidance was added to the iron management criteria, and a new exploratory objective
to compare the effect of daprodustat to rhEPO on delayed graft function (DGF) after deceased
donor kidney transplantation was added.
Amendment 02
Amendment 02 was implemented October 12, 2016 and applied to all countries and sites. It also
included country-specific requirements for France and Czech Republic. The main changes to the
protocol included the changes from country-specific Amendment 1. The section relating to
darbepoetin alfa pre-filled syringe incidents, malfunctions and user errors was deleted. The
Schedules of Assessments were modified to include new timepoints and prompts. Text was
added to clarify participants randomized to rhEPO who transition from hemodialysis to
peritoneal dialysis change from epoetin alfa to darbepoetin alfa. Text relating to the timing of
dialysis in relation to the study visit was amended. The negatively adjudicated events reporting
process was updated. Historical kidney ultrasound and a provision for a more sensitive imaging
study (e.g., MRI, CT) to assess entry criteria were added.
Amendment 03
Amendment 03 was implemented October 5, 2017 and applied to all countries and sites. The
main changes to the protocol included a provision for possible adjustment to the Dose
Adjustment Algorithm triggers for Hb values 7.5 g/dL to <9.5 g/dL based on the review of
blinded instream aggregate Hb data. Retest values for Hb and TSAT to determine eligibility at
Week -8 were added. Exclusion criteria were broadened. Guidance for those receiving
hemodialysis two times a week randomized to the epoetin alfa arm and to require three times a
week epoetin alfa dosing was added. A provision was added for unexpected circumstances where
the supply to the site was interrupted. New darbepoetin alfa dose strengths (not available in all
countries) were added. Directions regarding randomized treatment and study continuation for
participants away from the research site for an extended period of time were added.
Clarifications were made to the timeframe for iron management criteria, the “frequency of
dialysis” inclusion criterion, the valid study visit days by dialysis type, and the Baseline dose for
the purposes of the rescue algorithm for participants switching from hemodialysis to peritoneal

[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

dialysis who are randomized to rhEPO. Visit windows were revised and clarified. The FSH level
to confirm menopause was updated.
The Schedules of Assessments were modified for clarification and to include new activities and
prompts. The additional inclusion and eligible visits to collect PK samples in PK sub-studies
were clarified. The PRO section was updated to add healthcare resource utilization data.
The statistical section was revised to change from two-sided testing at the 5% level to one-sided
testing at the 2.5% level; to correct the comparator for the Null and Alternative hypotheses; to
change significance levels to p-values; to add a more complete description of the adjustments to
statistical model; to update hyporesponder analyses; and to add additional text regarding the
interim analysis process. Exploratory endpoints around Hb variability, iron parameters,
transfusions, and dose adjustment scheme were added. Adjustments to the objectives, endpoints
and analysis of the ABPM sub-study were made.
Amendment 04
Amendment 04 was implemented July 30, 2020 and applied to all countries and sites.
The protocol was modified to add retest values for Hb and TSAT in order to determine
eligibility. The MACE non-inferiority margin was revised; as a result, the target MACE was also
revised (to 664 events). The primary rationale for these changes was to accelerate the closeout of
the study in consideration of the COVID-19 pandemic and its impact on study integrity and data
quality. Additionally, this change was aligned with similar studies that had already compared the
effects of HIF-PHIs on MACE versus rhEPO, based on publicly available information. Analysis
of the Hb co-primary endpoint and the multiplicity adjustment strategy were updated.
Please note that the protocol amendment was approved after 664 events had already occurred
during the study. This means that some MACE related analyses contain more than 664 events.
Reviewer’s Comments
The amendments did not negatively impact the study design, conduct or results of the study.
Protocol Deviations
Significant protocol deviations were reported for 7% of the participants in the daprodustat arm
and 81% of participants in the rhEPO arm. The majority of protocol deviations were due to study
procedures/assessments (44%) of the total population (N=2964) and were balanced between
arms. The protocol deviations recorded likely did not have an impact on the interpretation of the
results.
Eight sites were closed due to ongoing GCP non-compliance and three of the 8 closed sites were
closed due to research conduct, which included suspected fraud. The data from these 3 sites were
included in the primary study displays and to assess the impact of the sites on the overall study
conclusion, a supportive analysis was conducted, excluding the data from these 3 sites. The
results from supportive analyses, conducted by the Applicant, did not show a significant impact
on the primary endpoints when all randomized participants were excluded from these sites.

16. Efficacy
16.1. Additional Efficacy Analyses
16.1.1. ASCEND-D
A supportive analysis was performed for the primary intent-to-treat analysis of postrandomization mean change in Hb between Baseline and over the Evaluation Period (EP),
repeating the analysis using a shorter EP (Week 28 to 36). For each study the lower boundary of
the 95% CI for the treatment difference was greater than the prospectively defined noninferiority margin of -0.75 g/dL, thus demonstrating non-inferiority of daprodustat (see Table
218). Results were consistent with the conclusion from the primary analysis.

16.1.3. Secondary Endpoints ASCEND-NHQ
Efficacy Results – Secondary Endpoints
For the analysis of post-randomization Hb change from Baseline to Week 28 daprodustat was
nominally superior to placebo. The adjusted mean treatment difference (daprodustat - placebo)
was 1.36 (95% CI: 1.16, 1.55; p<0.0001) (Table 222).

Permanently stopping randomized treatment due to meeting rescue criteria was reported in 2
participants (<1%) in the daprodustat group and in 26 participants (8%) in the placebo group.
There was a lower risk of stopping randomized treatment due to meeting rescue criteria with
daprodustat compared with placebo and this lower risk achieved nominal significance with a
hazard ratio of 0.07 (95% CI: 0.02, 0.30; one-sided p=0.0002) (Table 225).

Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; CSR, clinical study report; PY, person-year

More participants in the daprodustat group had an improvement in SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain
score ≥6 points from Baseline at Week 28 than those in the placebo group, and the treatment
difference between the groups favored daprodustat (see Table 60). However, the review team has
concern about the Applicant’s proposed improvement threshold (refer to Section 6.3.2 for more
details).

16.2. ASCEND-NHQ Statistical Analysis Plan
Sample Size Determination
Approximately 600 participants were planned to be randomized (300 per arm) to receive
daprodustat or placebo, who would be treated to achieve and maintain Hb between 11 and
12 g/dL.
Under the assumption that up to 10% of participants would withdraw from the study before
Week 24 (start of EP), 600 randomized participants would result in at least 540 evaluable
participants.
For the primary endpoint, the expected difference in mean Hb change from baseline and the EP,
between arms, was 1.0 g/dL and the anticipated between participant standard deviation was
1.5 g/dL, based on historical rhEPO trials and daprodustat clinical trial experience to date. The
planned study size would provide more than 99% power for superiority on Hb. In addition, it was
anticipated that the smallest difference in mean Hb change between arms that would meet the
statistical criterion for superiority would be 0.25 g/dL.
For the first key secondary endpoint of % of participants having a Hb increase of ≥1.0 g/dL from
baseline to EP, 540 evaluable participants would provide >90% power to detect a 20% difference
between daprodustat and placebo, assuming 30% of placebo arm participants would have a Hb
increase of ≥1.0 g/dL. In that case, the smallest difference between daprodustat and placebo that
would meet the statistical criterion for superiority would be 8%.
Missing Data Handling for Primary Efficacy Endpoint
Multiple imputation analysis was performed using all available Hb values (on and off-treatment)
and conducted under a set of assumptions about missing Hb values.

Jesduvroq (daprodustat)






For each of the missing dataset (out of the 200 imputed as indicated above), the missing
scheduled Hb values through Week 28 were imputed based on different scenarios. The
ANCOVA regression had region, treatment, baseline Hb, off treatment status, and prior
scheduled (possibly imputed) Hb values as covariates. Missing data were handled as
either being on-treatment or off treatment, with the former being imputed with only ontreatment data and the latter being imputed with only off-treatment data
- Missing data were classified as being on-treatment, if
 Patients died, used non-randomized ESA Medication, received blood
transfusions while on treatment
 Study Drug Interruption (e.g., temporarily stopped treatment due to
COVID, then restarted)
- Missing data were classified as being off-treatment, if
 Treatment discontinuation
 Patients died, used non-randomized ESA Medication, received blood
transfusions after treatment discontinuation
The low and high cutoff Hb values of 6 g/dL and 15 g/dL were applied to all imputed Hb
values.
EP Hb values were computed and compared across treatment groups using the primary
ANCOVA model described above. Rubin’s rules were used to combine results of the
imputed datasets using SAS PROC MIANALYZE procedure. As a result, a single
estimated treatment difference and its standard error was produced, with which a 95% CI
was calculated.

Analysis for Primary Efficacy Endpoint
The primary efficacy estimand was the effect of daprodustat treatment relative to placebo
regardless of adherence to treatment, the use of non-randomized ESA medication for any reason
including rescue therapy, or the use of blood transfusions, in participants defined by the
inclusion/exclusion criteria and assuming participants did not die before the end of the EP.
The primary efficacy endpoint was the mean change in Hb between baseline and over the EP
(mean of all available [on and off treatment] Hb values for Week 24 through Week 28, inclusive,
including any unscheduled Hb values that were taken during this time period). To quantify the
difference in mean Hb change, an ANCOVA model adjusted for treatment, baseline Hb, and
region was used. The primary model provided a two-sided 95% CI and one-sided superiority pvalue for the difference in the primary Hb endpoint between the daprodustat and placebo arm.
Superiority would be established if the one-sided p-value was less than 0.025.
Sensitivity Analysis
A sensitivity analysis that considers an “on-drug” efficacy estimand was conducted. In this
analysis, only Hb values measured while the participant was taking randomized study medication
was included. Missing Hb values were imputed based on the reasons for the missing Hb data.
Analyses for Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
For the endpoint of % of participants having a Hb increase of ≥1.0 g/dL from baseline to EP, the
EP Hb value was defined as the mean of all available Hb values (on- and off-treatment) during

[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

the EP (Week 24 to Week 28, inclusive). A CMH chi-squared test that adjusted for treatment and
region was used to make comparisons between the treatment groups. A treatment policy strategy
was used as in the primary analysis to include all on- and off-treatment Hb values of the ITT
populations, including values taken after discontinuation of study treatment/rescue therapy.
Missing data were imputed as described for the primary efficacy analysis.
For the key secondary endpoint of mean change in SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain score between
baseline and Week 28, a hypothetical strategy, including only on treatment values, was used
assuming participants had not had intercurrent events of death prior to the end of the EP and
randomized treatment discontinuation prior to the end of the EP. Data collected after
discontinuation of study treatment/rescue medication were not included in the analysis and were
treated as missing. The SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain scores after discontinuation of study
treatment were imputed based on the conservative assumption that there was no change in the
domain scores after the intercurrent event, so the last available on-treatment values (or baseline
values if there was no on-treatment value) were used in the imputation. Missing SF-36 v2.0
Vitality domain scores were imputed based on a missing at random assumption. An ANCOVA
model was used including factors for baseline score, treatment, and region. The analysis was
performed for both 0-100 scoring and norm-based scoring. The 0-100 scoring was considered as
the primary results and norm-based as supportive results.
Additional Secondary Endpoints
For the Hb responder analysis, mean Hb during the EP was defined as the on-drug supportive
analysis. Responders were participants with a mean Hb during the EP that fell within the Hb
analysis range of 11 to 12 g/dL. A CMH chi-squared test that adjusted for treatment and region
was used to compare the number and % of responders between the treatment groups. The onesided CMH p-value was compared to 0.025 to assess nominal significance.
For the time to stopping study treatment due to meeting rescue criteria, a Cox proportional
hazards model adjusted for the baseline categorical values of treatment and region was used.
Confidence intervals (CIs) for the rate per 100 person-years was also reported. For within-group
rates, the two-sided 95% CI was obtained using an exact Poisson method. For difference in rates
between treatments, the two-sided 95% CI was constructed with a normal approximation using
Wald’s method. One-sided p-values were compared to 0.025 to assess nominal significance.
Secondary endpoints included the mean change from baseline in Patient Global Impression of
Severity (PGI-S) score and each Chronic Kidney Disease Anemia Questionnaire (CKD-AQ)
domain score and single items were analyzed using a mixed model repeated measures approach
with an unstructured covariance matrix to compare the difference in means between arms. The
models were fitted to data collected after Baseline up to Week 28, using on-treatment values and
excluded the measures taken after discontinuation of study treatment. The models included
factors for treatment, time, region, treatment by time interaction, corresponding baseline score
values (i.e., PGI-S score, CKD-AQ domain, and CKD-AQ single items score in the
corresponding model), and the baseline score by time interaction. Analyses were done with the
MIXED procedure in SAS, using the Kenward-Roger option to estimate denominator degrees of
freedom and standard errors. A point estimate and two-sided 95% CI for the adjusted mean
treatment difference (daprodustat - placebo) and a one-sided superiority p-value for this test at
