Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

Week 28 were provided. One-sided p-values were compared to 0.025 to assess nominal
significance.
For the % of participants having an improvement in SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain score ≥6 from
baseline at Week 28, a CMH chi-squared test that adjusted for treatment and region was used to
make comparisons between the treatment groups. This was based on the 0-100 scoring. As with
the second key secondary endpoint, on-treatment SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain scores were used
and excluded the measures taken after discontinuation of study treatment.
The mean change from baseline in SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain scores (individual items of the
Vitality domain, overall score of the Physical Function domain), EuroQol 5 Dimension 5 Level
(EQ-5D-5L) score, and EuroQol Visual Analogue Scale (EQ-VAS) score were analyzed using a
mixed model repeated measures approach with an unstructured covariance matrix to compare the
difference in means between arms. The models were fitted to HRQoL data collected after
Baseline up to Week 28, using on treatment values and excluded the measures taken after
discontinuation of study treatment. The model included factors for treatment, time, region,
treatment by time interaction, corresponding baseline value (HRQoL parameter or utility scores),
and the baseline value by time interaction term.
Multiple Comparison
The primary endpoint based on mean change from baseline on Hb value was tested first for
superiority using a one-sided 2.5% significance level. Conditional on achieving statistical
significance, the two key secondary endpoints would be tested for superiority using a one-sided
2.5% significance level sequentially. The additional secondary/exploratory endpoints were not
adjusted for multiplicity. Summary statistics and nominal one-sided 2.5% significance levels
were used to describe the results of these treatment comparisons.
Subgroup Analysis
Subgroup analysis including age, sex, and race was performed using all available Hb values (onand off- treatment, including imputed values) and separately using evaluable Hb values only.
The ANCOVA models used in the primary analysis was used and adjusted for treatment,
baseline Hb, region, subgroup, and subgroup treatment interaction term.
For the first key secondary endpoint (% of participants having a Hb increase of ≥1.0 g/dL from
baseline to EP), subgroup analysis was performed using all available Hb values (on- and offtreatment). A CMH chi-squared test that adjusted for treatment and subgroup was used to make
comparisons between the treatment groups.
For the second key secondary endpoint, subgroup analyses were performed using an ANCOVA
model that included factors for baseline score, treatment, region, subgroup, and subgroup
treatment interaction.

16.2.1. Review of the 36-item Short Form Health
Survey version 2.0 (SF-36 v2.0) Vitality Domain
for Evaluation of Clinical Benefit
The Applicant used the SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain to assess the secondary efficacy PRO
endpoint in ASCEND-NHQ. This instrument was reviewed for content validity, other
measurement properties, and meaningful within-patient change.
16.2.1.1. Instrument Description
The SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain (Figure 64 in Section 16.3.1) is a 4-item domain within the SF36 v2.0, which is designed to assess some aspects of fatigue related to fullness of life, energy,
feeling tired, and feeling worn out. Each item is rated on a 5-point verbal rating scale ranging
from “None of the time” to “All of the time.” The recall period is the previous 7 days (“past
week”).
Each item score of the SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain ranges between 1 to 5. The four items in the
SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain are first summed to calculate a raw domain score that ranges from 4
to 20. Of note, the positively worded items (feeling “full of life” and having energy) are reverse
scored (i.e., a response value of 1 corresponds to a raw score value of 5). The raw domain score
is then converted to a 0-100 scaled score by the formula shown below, where higher scores
indicate better health, and lower scores indicate more disability (or worse health state):
0-100 scaled SF-36 Vitality score = ((SF-36 Vitality domain raw sum score) – 4)/16*100)
To limit missing data at the item-level, the SF-36 v2.0 was administered in an electronic format
and respondents were not permitted to skip items.
16.2.1.2. Content Validity
The Applicant completed the following instrument development activities to evaluate the content
validity of the SF-36 Vitality domain in patients with anemia associated with CKD:



A literature review
Patient input (published qualitative study, reanalysis of existing patient interview
transcripts)

The review team evaluated the data generated from these instrument development activities.
Both literature and patient input (from published qualitative study and reanalysis of patient
interview transcripts) confirmed that the content of the SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain was relevant
to the patient experience with anemia due to CKD. While the SF-36 v2.0 has been widely used
across different diseases, the Applicant did not cognitively test the SF-36 v2.0 with patients with
anemia due to CKD to evaluate patient comprehension. However, the Applicant cited a
published qualitative study that conducted cognitive interviews in patients with type 2 diabetes
and anemia due to CKD; this literature provides limited details regarding patient comprehension
of the SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain.

16.2.1.3. Other Measurement Properties (Reliability,
Construct Validity, Responsiveness)
The Applicant evaluated the other measurement properties of the SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain
using combined data from two phase 3 trials in adult patients with anemia associated with CKD:



ASCEND-ND (n=350): A randomized, open-label, active controlled, parallel-group,
multi-center, event-driven study in non-dialysis subjects with anemia associated with
CKD.
ASCEND-ID (n=100): A randomized, open-label, active-controlled, parallel-group,
multi-center study in subjects with anemia associated with CKD who were not routine
ESA users and were initiating dialysis.

The Applicant’s rationale for combining the data from the two trials was to ensure a full
representation in the sample among a broad range of patients with CKD across the continuum of
the disease—from non-dialysis to those who were previously not on dialysis, but subsequently
required the initiation of dialysis. It’s unclear why data from ASCEND-D were not included as
well.
Two ‘refresh samples21’ were used to evaluate the measurement properties of the SF-36 v2.0
Vitality domain. Both refresh samples 1 and 2 comprised 350 ASCEND-ND participants. A
further 100 ASCEND-ID participants were also included in refresh sample 1. Of the 100
ASCEND-ID participants included in refresh sample 1, 50 were randomly selected to also be
included in refresh sample 2. For more details regarding the refresh samples, refer to Section 2.2
in Appendix 20 of the Applicant’s SF-36 PRO Dossier (“EVA-28889-01: Confirmation of the
Psychometric Properties of the SF-36 Vitality Scale Among Patients with Chronic Kidney
Disease” report Version 1.0 dated July 26, 2021).
Based on FDA’s assessment, the quantitative sample used for psychometric analyses is not fully
comparable to the study population of ASCEND-NHQ. Despite this, the reliability (internal
consistency and test-retest reliability) and convergent validity of the SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain
appear to be within acceptable and reasonable ranges. The results for assessment of known
groups validity (a form of construct validity) were difficult to interpret as the Applicant’s
selected known groups (i.e., Hb, CKD stage, dialysis status, PGI-S, CKD-AQ Items 1 and 2) did
not adequately categorize patients by the severity of fatigue-related symptoms. The Applicant
did not evaluate the responsiveness of the SF-36 v2.0 Vitality domain using the refresh samples.
Instead, the Applicant cited publications to support the responsiveness of this instrument. The
review team acknowledges that the responsiveness of this instrument has been widely published,
however, it remains difficult to fully evaluate the responsiveness of this instrument solely on
literature given the considerable variability observed in the context of use (e.g., design of
studies).
21 The samples are termed ‘refresh’ as they were also used to confirm psychometric properties of the CKD-AQ.

(b) (4)

17. Clinical Safety
17.1. Missing Data Sensitivity Analyses
The Applicant conducted pre-specified tipping point sensitivity analyses under a range of
missing data assumptions, in addition to non-informative censoring, to determine the
assumptions that would change the non-inferiority conclusions for MACE. Assumptions about
missing outcomes in the daprodustat and control arms varied independently and included
scenarios where subjects with missing data on daprodustat had worse outcomes than subjects
with missing data on control. Patients who prematurely withdrew22 from the study without a
known first MACE prior to study withdrawal were multiply imputed in bootstrap samples under
22 Withdrawal includes withdrawal of consent, lost to follow-up, and study site closure

352
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

a range of event rate scenarios. As requested by the FDA during the NDA review, the follow-up
period for all subjects was from randomization to the date of study completion/withdrawal,
excluding deaths after this time.
For each treatment arm, the assumed event rates ranged from all prematurely censored subjects
had a MACE at the time of censoring to all prematurely censored subjects completed the study
without experiencing the event. These event rates, including a censored-at-random scenario,
were quantified using the log-hazard rate ratio comparing prematurely censored subjects to
observed subjects (Jackson et al. 2014). For each pair (daprodustat, control) of log-hazard rate
ratio, the imputed time to first occurrence of MACE in a bootstrap sample was then compared
between treatment groups using the primary analysis Cox proportional hazards model.23 Rubin’s
rules combined the result (hazard ratio) of the Cox proportional hazards model for 200 imputed
datasets. HR estimates were reported with the assumed hazard rate ratio and number of events
for the missing subjects in each treatment arm. Interpretation of sensitivity analysis results
required an assessment of the plausibility of the assumptions regarding: (1) missing subjects’
event rate relative to observed subjects’ event rate in each treatment arm; and (2) missing
subjects’ event rate in the daprodustat arm relative to the control arm.
Using the same imputation method, the Applicant also explored the impact of missing secondary
safety endpoint information for the adjudicated endpoints: all-cause mortality, CV mortality, MI,
stroke, hospitalization for heart failure, and thromboembolic event. Unlike MACE, for which
missing data assumptions that changed (tipped) the non-inferiority conclusion could be
identified, secondary safety endpoints were tested but not controlled for multiple comparisons;
therefore, the determination of which missing data assumptions were plausible and would result
in a concerning hazard ratio required an abundance of judgement, because there was not a
multiplicity controlled test as a point of reference. In that regard, the interpretation of sensitivity
analyses for secondary safety endpoints were limited and not presented. Only MACE tipping
point analyses results are shown.
ASCEND-ND
In the primary analysis of MACE, 61/1937 (3%) daprodustat and 61/1935 (3%) darbepoetin alfa
subjects were censored. The time to event analysis results of MACE, with deaths excluded after
study completion/withdrawal, was HR 1.05 (CI 0.93, 1.21)24 with incidence rates of: daprodustat
367/1937 (19%); darbepoetin alfa 356/1935 (18%). Note that the HR estimate is slightly
different from the pre-specified primary analysis result of MACE that included deaths after study
completion/withdrawal (see discussion in Section 7.4). Sensitivity analyses (Figure 68) to the
non-informative censoring assumption in the MACE primary analysis showed that the results
(HR 1.09; 95% CI 0.4, 1.26) tipped, i.e., did not rule out the 1.25 risk margin, when daprodustattreated, censored subjects were assumed to have 19/61 (31%) MACE and a (worse) hazard rate
of 1.7 times the daprodustat-treated, observed subjects, and darbepoetin alfa-treated, censored
23 Primary analysis of time to first MACE was a Cox proportional hazards model controlling for treatment and

adjusting for the following baseline variables used in stratified randomization: region (Asia Pacific, Eastern
Europe/South Africa, Western Europe/Canada/ANZ, Latin America, USA), current ESA use (ASCEND-ND only:
User, Non-user), and dialysis type (ASCEND-D only: HD, peritoneal dialysis).

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

subjects were assumed to have 4/61 (7%) MACE and a (better) hazard rate of 0.4 times the
darbepoetin alfa-treated, observed subjects. A less extreme assumption (daprodustat 1.7,
darbepoetin alfa 0.6) resulted in HR 1.08 (95% CI 0.93, 1.25), which did not tip the MACE
results away from non-inferiority (Figure 69, estimate 1.08 boxed in green). Note that assuming
a hazard rate ratio of 1.0 in each arm is equivalent to the non-informative censoring assumption.
Comment: The MACE rate assumptions in the daprodustat and darbepoetin alfa arms (1.7 vs 0.4
times the event rate of observed subjects) that censored darbepoetin alfa-treated subjects have a
lower MACE rate than the observed darbepoetin alfa-treated subjects could be considered
implausible for a tipping point in an active-controlled study. A limitation of these analyses was
that the Applicant did not consider patients that failed to have a CV endpoint assessment during
or after the EOS window to be missing outcome data. Although including these subjects could
increase the number of subjects missing to be approximately 5%25 in each treatment arm, missing
data were low and similar across arms. The primary analysis result for MACE, assuming noninformative censoring, was tipped only under fairly extreme assumptions of daprodustat having
more MACE while the active control having less MACE than the observed subjects. Therefore,
the primary analysis assuming non-informative censoring could be relied upon for interpretation.

of subjects missing to be approximately 11%27 in each treatment arm, however, the rates of
missing subjects were similar across the arms. In summary, the primary analysis result for

daprodustat having more MACE while the active control having less MACE than the observed
subjects. Therefore, the primary analysis assuming non-informative censoring could be relied

17.3. CV Events in Relation to Average Dose
Summaries Exploratory Analyses
We explored the relationship between dose level and each adjudicated CV endpoint in studies
ASCEND-D and ASCEND-ND. The endpoints included MACE, all-cause mortality, CV
mortality, myocardial infarction, stroke, hospitalization for heart failure, and thromboembolic
events. For subjects who experience more than one adverse event with the same preferred term
or more than one adverse event within a grouping of preferred terms, the first adverse event was
used for analysis, and exposure time used in the calculation of total patient-years was censored at
the date of the first adverse event. We focused on the dosing frequency adjusted on-treatment CV
period, which is the endpoint ascertainment window defined as the period starting from the
358
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

treatment start date to the earlier of the date of study completion/withdrawal or last non-zero
dose date + dosing frequency28 + 28 days (OT + Dosing Frequency + 28).
Dose was analyzed by:





Average dose while receiving treatment
Average dose received during the 4 weeks preceding the event
Average dose received during the 2 weeks preceding the event
Average dose at the time of the event

In “average dose while receiving treatment” analysis, overall average weekly study drug dose
was calculated for each subject until the first occurrence of the event for subjects with the event
or until the end of the ascertainment window for subjects without the event. Each of these overall
averages were assigned to one of five fixed dose categories.29 The number of events were
tabulated by for each dose category in each treatment group and expressed as a percent of
subjects with events.
Because these drugs were titrated to effect throughout the treatment period, alternative analyses
were conducted using a moving average of the dose. In “average dose received during the
4 weeks preceding the event” analysis, average weekly dose during the 4 weeks prior to the
event of interest was calculated for every subject until the first occurrence of the event of interest
for subjects with the event or until the end of the ascertainment window for subjects without the
event. For example, the dose at Week 16 was the average dose during Weeks 13 through 16. The
average weekly dose was assigned to one of five fixed dose categories. A similar analysis was
conducted using two-week intervals preceding the event, which was referred to as “average dose
received during the 2 weeks preceding the event.” In “average dose at the time of the event”
analysis, the weekly dose at the time of the event was defined as the dose on the day the event
occurred, converted to a weekly dose, and assigned to a dose category. In these three analyses,
the count of subjects with an event, total person-years (PY), rate per PY and rate per 100 PY
were calculated for each dose category and each treatment group.
Summary tables and figures were created for each endpoint by treatment arm (daprodustat,
control) for each study (ASCEND-ND, ASCEND-D). Results are not shown.
Summary: Higher percentages or incidence rates observed at higher dose categories for some CV
endpoints including MACE in both treatment arms. Limitations with the interpretability of these
analyses included: (1) potential bias in treatment (daprodustat vs. control) effect comparison
using an on-treatment analysis that has the potential to break the integrity of randomization and
introduce bias as there may differences between treatment arms in the types of subjects that
adhere to treatment; (2) potential confounding by disease severity in the relationship between CV

28 1 day (daprodustat); 7, 14, or 28 days (darbepoetin alfa); 2 or 7 days (epoetin alfa).
29 Sponsor determined the dose categories based on the possible doses in each treatment arm of each study. Dose

categories differed by arm and can differ by the dose categorization (while receiving treatment, 4 weeks preceding
the event, 2 weeks preceding the event, at the time of event). Categories were chosen to “balance the representation
of a range of doses across the categories, while having sufficient amount of observation time in each dose category
to permit meaningful examination of the data.”

event by dose level; and (3) data sparsity for most events except for MACE and all-cause
mortality.

17.4. CV Events in Relation to Average
Hemoglobin Summaries
We explored the relationship between hemoglobin (Hb) level and each adjudicated CV endpoint
in studies ASCEND-D and ASCEND-ND. The endpoints included MACE, all-cause mortality,
CV mortality, myocardial infarction, stroke, hospitalization for heart failure, and
thromboembolic events. For subjects who experience more than one adverse event with the same
preferred term or more than one adverse event within a grouping of preferred terms, the first
adverse event was used for analysis, and exposure time used in the calculation of total patientyears was censored at the dated of the first adverse event. We focused on the dosing frequency
adjusted on-treatment CV period, which is the endpoint ascertainment window defined as the
period starting from the treatment start date to the earlier of the date of study
completion/withdrawal or last non-zero dose date + dosing frequency30 + 28 days (OT + Dosing
Frequency + 28).
Hb was analyzed by:



Hb just prior to the event
Hb rate of change over the 4 weeks preceding the event




Separate analysis for rates of increases and decreases

Hb rate of change over the 12 weeks preceding the event


Separate analysis for rates of increases and decreases

For each treatment arm, Hb just prior to the event of interest was defined as the last on-treatment
Hb just prior to the event. This was calculated for every subject until the first occurrence of the
event for subjects with the event or until end of treatment of subjects without the event. The
number of events were tabulated for each Hb quintile, determined from all Hb values, indicating
how many subjects had an event in that quintile for their given Hb associated with the event.
Total PY, rate per PY, and rate per 100 PY were calculated for each quintile.
Another Hb analysis explored the Hb rate of change (g/dL/week) over the 4 weeks preceding the
event. Using the subject’s treatment start date as the anchor date, a 4-week interval was created
up to either the event of interest or the end of the ascertainment window if they did not have an
event. Then a Hb slope was calculated by fitting a linear regression line through the Hb values
obtained during the 4 weeks. For example, the slope at Week 8 was calculated from Hb values
obtained on Weeks 5, 6, 7, 8. The slopes were categorized as either positive (including zero) or
negative slopes. Quintiles were created based on the positive slopes and separately, for the
negative slopes. The number of events, total PY, rate per PY, and rate per 100 PY were
calculated for each Hb quintile. The analyses were repeated for rate of change over the 12 weeks
30 1 day (daprodustat); 7, 14, or 28 days (darbepoetin alfa); 2 or 7 days (epoetin alfa).

360
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5119138

NDA 216951
Jesduvroq (daprodustat)

preceding the event. Of note, Hb slopes could be only estimated for a limited number of events,
because Hb assessments were scheduled less frequently later in the trials (at least every 4 weeks
during the first year of the trial and at least every 12 weeks thereafter), and two values were
necessary to calculate a slope.
Summary tables and figures were created for each endpoint by treatment arm (daprodustat,
control) for each study (ASCEND-ND, ASCEND-D). Results are not shown.
Summary: Higher incidence rates observed at lower Hb quintiles for some CV endpoints
including MACE in both treatment arms. Limitations with the interpretability of these analyses
included: (1) potential bias in treatment (daprodustat vs. control) effect comparison using an ontreatment analysis that has the potential to break the integrity of randomization and introduce
bias as there may be differences between treatment arms in the types of subjects that adhere to
treatment; (2) potential confounding by disease severity in the relationship between CV event by
Hb level; and (3) data sparsity for most events except for MACE and all-cause mortality.

17.5. Subgroup Analyses by ESA Use – ASCENDND
To further explore CV endpoints among baseline ESA users versus non-users in the NDD
population, exploratory subgroup analyses were conducted for each adjudicated CV endpoint.
For all these CV endpoints, a higher incidence was observed with daprodustat compared to
darbepoetin alfa among ESA users and non-users at randomization, except CV MACE and MI
among ESA non-users (Figure 72). The variability (CI widths) of HR estimates depended on the
number of events and sample sizes, with lower precision for endpoints like stroke and
thromboembolic events. In summary, analyses of ESA use subgroups did not indicate a
consistently higher risk among ESA users compared to non-users, but the elevated HR estimates
in both subgroups across multiple CV endpoints corroborated the unfavorable results in the
overall study population.

22. Data Integrity–Related Consults (Office of
Scientific Investigations, Other
Inspections)
Data quality was ensured by the Applicant through periodic monitoring with primary source
verification of trial data at all trial sites. Throughout the application review period, data quality
was evaluated by the clinical review team, who independently reviewed the provided datasets
and noted no anomalies in enrollment characteristics, patterns of protocol violations reported,
patterns of efficacy reporting or patterns of SAE reporting.

Consults were obtained from the Office of Scientific Investigations and Office of Study Integrity
and Surveillance and described below.
Office of Clinical Investigations
Four clinical investigator sites (Drs. Barton Cox Brezina, Anjay Rastogi, Chao Sun and Kendra
Hendon) were inspected for Studies 200807/ASCEND-D and 200808/ASCEND-ND.
From the review by Anthony Orencia M.D., Ph.D., F.A.C.P., Min Lu, M.D., M.P.H. and Jenn
Sellers, M.D., Ph.D. “Based on clinical inspections, the study data derived from the above
clinical investigator sites appear reliable. The study data submitted to the Agency for assessment
are acceptable in support of the proposed indication.”
Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance (OSIS)
From the review “The Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance (OSIS) arranged the inspection
of study 213022 (NDA 216951) conducted at Altasciences Clinical Kansas, Inc. and PPD
Development, L.P.
At Altasciences Clinical Kansas, Inc, a Form FDA 483 was not issued at the close-out of the
inspection. However, there was verbal discussion with site’s management regarding the
maintenance of blinding codes onsite. The discussion item did not impact the reliability of the
data from the audited study. At PPD Development, L.P., no objectionable conditions were
observed, and a Form FDA 483 was not issued at its inspection close-out.”
The review concluded that the data for study 213022, “ A Two-Part, Randomized, Double-Blind,
Single-Dose, Crossover Study to Compare Daprodustat Formulations Produced by Two Methods
of Manufacture for Bioequivalence and Dissolution in Healthy Adult Volunteers” from both
clinical sites are reliable.

to ensure that PI meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if appropriate) with
labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate information needed
for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise information for the
healthcare practitioner.

The summary analyses should include an assessment of all new information obtained during the

1. Background
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in rats
and one in mice. These studies were to determine the carcinogenic potential of GSK1278863A, a
HIF-prolyl hydroxylase inhibitor, when given once daily by oral gavage for up to 104
consecutive weeks to Crl:CD (SD) rats, and once daily by oral gavage and subcutaneous
injection of a cocktail formulation containing the three major circulating human metabolites for a
minimum of 104 weeks to Crl:CD1 (ICR) mice, respectively.
In this review the phrase "dose response relationship" refers to the linear component (trend) of the
effect of treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor
incidence rate as dose increases.
2. Rat Study
Two separate experiments, one in male rats and one in female rats were conducted. As indicated
in Table 1, in each of these two experiments there were four treated groups, and one vehicle
control group. Three hundred and fifty Crl:CD (SD) rats of each sex were assigned randomly in
size of 70 rats per group. The dose levels for the four treated groups were 0.02, 0.2, 0.8, and 4
mg/kg/day for male rats, and 0.02, 0.2, 0.8, and 7 mg/kg/day for female rats, respectively. In this
review these dose groups were referred to as the low (Group 2), mid (Group 3), mid-high
(Group4), and high (Group 5) dose groups, respectively. The rats in the vehicle control groups
were administrated with 1% (w/v) methyl cellulose (400 cP@ 2%) with 0.2% Tween 80 (v/v)
with 5 mM NaOH in Ultra Pure Water, and handled for the same duration and in the same
manner as the treated groups.
In view of the fact that the number of survivors had reached 15 in the high dose male rats (Group
5, 4 mg/kg/day) on Day 650, and had reached 20 in the Reference Item group on Days 675 and
695 for male and female rats, respectively, early study termination was initiated on Day 653 for
Group 5 of male rats, Day 679 for Group 1 to Group 4 of male rats and on Day 698 for all female
rats.

A viability check was performed near the start and end of each working day for main study
animals. A necropsy was performed on those animals that died or were euthanized during the
dosing period. Detailed examinations were performed on all Main Study animals weekly starting
during the last week of the prestudy period and on the day of necropsy. All animals found dead
during the study were subjected to necropsy, and tissue samples were preserved. Main study

Reference ID: 5015782

NDA 216951 (GSK1278863A)

Page 4

animals were subjected to a complete necropsy examination, which included evaluation of the
carcass and musculoskeletal system; all external surfaces and orifices; cranial cavity and external
surfaces of the brain; and thoracic, abdominal, and pelvic cavities with their associated organs
and tissues.
2.1.
2.1.1.

Sponsor's analyses

Survival analysis

In the sponsor’s report, each animal survival time was measured by the number of study days
until its death. The animal was classified as uncensored when death occurred within the
experimental period and was not accidental. Otherwise, that is, when death was accidental, as it
is the case for animals’ nos. 3016 and 4524, and/or occurred during the terminal sacrifice period,
the animal was classified as censored. The first day of the terminal sacrifice period was
considered as the censoring time of all terminal animals.
For each sex, Kaplan-Meier product-limit method, applied on daily intervals, was used to
estimate each group survival function for the dosing period and to generate the corresponding
survival curves for males and females. At the 5% level, the significance of a dose-related trend in
mortality across the reference item group (Group 1) and the four test item treated groups (Groups
2 to 5) was evaluated for each sex via Peto’s two-sided test (Peto et al., 1980), using the
corresponding arithmetic dose level scores. For the females, the doses are 0, 0.02, 0.1, 0.8, and 7
mg/kg/day. However, for the males, given that dosing stopped after Day 670 for Groups 1 to 4
and at Day 631 for Group 5, and given that the first day of sacrifice was Day 679 for Groups 1 to
4 and Day 653 for Group 5, the doses 0, 0.02, 0.1, 0.8, and 4 mg/kg/day were adjusted
accordingly and, the adjusted doses 0, 0.0198, 0.0988, 0.7906, and 3.8712 mg/kg/day were used.
In addition, Peto’s two-tailed trend test was conducted, at the 5% significance level, to compare
the mortality rates in the reference item group and in each of the test item treated group.
Sponsor’s findings:
The sponsor’s analysis showed that the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy were
20 (29%), 17 (24%), 17 (24%), 16 (23%), and 15 (21%) in the vehicle control, the low, mid, midhigh, and high dose groups for male rats, respectively, and 20 (29%), 21 (30%), 20 (29%), 27
(39%), and 30 (43%) for female rats respectively. For female rats, the trend and the pairwise
comparisons test results were not found to be significant. For male rats, the trend test result
revealed a significant increase in the mortality rate across doses, with p-value = 0.0025, and the
pairwise comparison results revealed that the mortality rate in the high dose group (Group 5) is
significantly higher than the one in the vehicle control group (Group 1), with p-value = 0.0131.
2.1.2.

Tumor data analysis

In the sponsor’s analysis, the statistical evaluation was done for each sex separately and was
limited to all non-secondary neoplastic lesions found in study plan-required tissues/sites as well
as to the combined hemangiosarcomas across whole body. Tumors listed under subcutis and
hemolymphoreticular tissue were statistically analyzed using all study animals. Tumors listed

Reference ID: 5015782

NDA 216951 (GSK1278863A)

Page 5

under tissues/sites that were not systematically examined for all animals were excluded from the
statistical analysis.
For male rats, given that the last day of dosing was Day 670 for Groups 1 to 4 and Day 631 for
Group 5, and given that the first day of terminal sacrifice was Day 679 for animals in Groups 1
to 4 and Day 653 for animals in Group 5, two separate analyses were considered. More precisely,
the first one included all groups with a common experimental period of 652 days and with doses
0, 0.02, 0.1, and 0.8 mg/kg/day for Groups 1 to 4 respectively, and adjusted dose of 3.8712
mg/kg/day for Group 5. The second analysis included Groups 1 to 4, with a common
experimental period of 678 days and with respective adjusted doses of 0, 0.0198, 0.0988, and
0.7906 mg/kg/day. For female rats, given that animals in all groups have been dosed during the
common experimental period of 697 days, only one analysis, with all groups and respective
doses 0, 0.02, 0.1, 0.8, and 7 mg/kg/day, was conducted. In addition to the overall trend test,
pairwise groups comparisons were made using Peto’s one-sided test to determine if the tumor
rate in each of the test item treated group is significantly higher than the one in the reference item
group.
Neoplastic lesions designated as palpable and listed under clitoral, preputial and mammary
glands, and under skin and subcutis were statistically analyzed according to Peto’s onset rate
method for tumors observed in a “mortality independent” context (Peto et al., 1980), using all
animals. The onset time of each palpable tumor was given by the first date of detection. The
death time was used as the onset time for a lesion categorized as palpable and not detected invivo. The death time of all animals dying after the end of the experimental period will be
considered to be the first day following the experimental period.
For combinations of both palpable and non-palpable tumors, the animal death time was used in
the analysis which was conducted according to the “mortality dependent” method described
hereafter for non palpable tumors. Tumors categorized as non-palpable were analyzed using the
“mortality dependent” method. In this context, each neoplastic finding was classified as the
cause of death (designated by the study pathologist as definitely or probably fatal) or not the
cause of death (designated by the study pathologist as definitely or probably incidental or
undetermined). However, all non-palpable neoplasms found in an animal were automatically
classified as incidental if the animal in question died after the experimental period. For
combinations of neoplastic findings, if an animal had one of the related tumors classified as fatal,
then the neoplastic finding defined by this combination was classified as fatal. Otherwise, it was
classified as incidental. Neoplastic findings classified as fatal and incidental were processed
using the death rate method and the prevalence method, respectively.
As per FDA’s draft guidance (FDA’s draft Guidance for industry, 2001), the processing of
incidental tumors was done by creating a single separate interval for the scheduled terminal
sacrifice period and by dividing the experimental period into the following fixed intervals: Days
0-350, Days 351-560, Days 561-652, and Day 653 and over for male rats first analysis, Days 0350, Days 351-560, Days 561-678, and Day 679 and over for male rats second analysis, and
Days 0-350, Days 351-560, Days 561- 697, and Day 698 and over for female rats analysis.

Reference ID: 5015782

NDA 216951 (GSK1278863A)

Page 6

Using the derived outcomes from the processing of both fatal and incidental tumors, a test
statistic was built to perform a global survival-adjusted trend test on tumor data observed in a
“mortality dependent” context (Peto et al., 1980). For each dataset of interest within each sex, the
significance of a linear dose-related increase in tumor occurrence rates across the reference item
group and test item treated groups was evaluated using Peto's survival-adjusted one-tailed trend
test. The related arithmetic dose level scores, multiplied by 10 to the power 4 for male rats and 2
for female rats, were used to perform the overall trend test.
As mentioned by Lin (Lin, 1997), the discrete permutation distribution was used to compute the
corresponding p-value for each statistical test performed on a dataset containing 10 or less tumor
occurrences. The interpretation of the results should be accomplished with the consideration of
both historical control data and biological relevance. In fact, the evaluation of the carcinogenic
potential of the test item should be undertaken in the light of an overall scientific judgment.
Adjustment for multiple testing:
In the sponsor’s report, according to the recommendations of Lin and Rahman (Lin and Rahman,
1998), the dose-related increase in tumor incidence should be considered significant if the pvalue is less than or equal to 0.025 for rare tumors and less than or equal to 0.005 for common
tumors (historical incidence of more than 1%); the increased tumor rate in a test item treated
group when compared with the reference item group is considered to be significant when the pvalue is less than or equal to 0.05 for a rare tumor, or is less than or equal to 0.01 for a common
tumor.
Sponsor’s findings:
In the sponsor’s report, the overall trend results from the analysis of male rats data excluding
Group 5, were not found to be significant (p-value > 0.05), whereas the overall trend results from
the analysis of male rats data and female rats data including all groups, revealed significance (pvalue ≤ 0.05) for the datasets listed in Table 2.
Table 2. Significant Findings (p-value ≤ 0.05) of the Overall Trend Results

Based on the adjustment criteria for multiple testing described in the sponsor’s report, for the
male rats first analysis including the high dose group (group 5), the results corresponding to
fibroadenoma under mammary gland will be considered to be statistically significant regardless
of this tumor type classification (rare or common), and the results corresponding to

Reference ID: 5015782

NDA 216951 (GSK1278863A)

Page 7

hemangiosarcoma under spleen and to adenoma under stomach will be considered to be
statistically significant only if these tumors are classified as rare. For female rats, the result
corresponding to fibroadenoma under mammary gland will be considered to be statistically
significant only if this tumor is classified as rare, and the result corresponding to histiocytic
sarcoma under hemolymphoreticular tissue will not be considered to be statistically significant.
The pairwise comparisons results revealed significance (p-value≤0.05) for the datasets listed in
Table 3. Based on the adjustment criteria for multiple testing described in the sponsor’s report,
for the male rats first analysis including the high dose group (group 5), mammary gland
fibroadenoma incidence rate in Group 5 will be considered to be significantly higher than the one
in Group 1 regardless of this tumor type classification (rare or common), and for male rats first
and second analyses, thyroid gland c-cell adenoma incidence rate in Group 4 will be considered
to be significantly higher than the one in Group 1 only if this tumor is classified as rare. For
female rats, both mammary gland fibroadenoma incidence rate in Group 5 and uterus
endometrial stromal polyp incidence rate in Group 3 will be considered to be significantly higher
than the one in Group 1 only if these tumors are classified as rare.

2.2.

Reviewer's analyses

To verify the sponsor’s analyses and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing
toxicologist, this reviewer independently performed the survival and tumor data analyses using
the data provided by the sponsor electronically.
2.2.1.

Survival analysis

In the reviewer’s analysis, the survival distributions of rats in all five groups (Groups 1, 2, 3, 4, and
5) were estimated using the Kaplan-Meier product limit method. The dose response relationship was
tested across Groups 1, 2, 3, 4, and 5 using the likelihood ratio test, and the homogeneity of survival
distributions was tested using the log-rank test. The Kaplan-Meier curves for survival rates are

Reference ID: 5015782

NDA 216951 (GSK1278863A)

Page 8

given in Figures 1A and 1B in the appendix for all five groups in male and female rats, respectively.
The intercurrent mortality data of all five groups and the results of the tests for dose response
relationship and homogeneity of survivals for Groups 1, 2, 3, 4, and 5 are given in Tables 1A and
1B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively.
Reviewer’s findings:
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 20 (29%), 17 (24%), 17 (24%), 16 (23%), and 15 (21%) in the vehicle control, the low, mid,
mid-high, and high dose groups for male rats, respectively, and 20 (29%), 21 (30%), 20 (29%),
27 (39%), and 30 (43%) for female rats respectively. A statistically significant increasing trend
in mortality was noted for male rats (p-value = 0.0040), with a corresponding statistically
significant increase in the high dose group when compared to the vehicle control group (p-values
= 0.0143). No other statistically significant dose response relationship and pairwise comparisons
in mortality was noted for male and female rats.
2.2.2.

Tumor data analysis

The tumor data were analyzed for dose response relationships across the vehicle control group, and
low, mid, mid-high, and high dose groups, and pairwise comparisons of each of the four treated
groups against the vehicle control group, using the Poly-k method described in the paper of Bailer
and Portier (1988) and Bieler and Williams (1993).
In the ploy-k method, the adjustment for differences in mortality among treatment groups is
made by modifying the number of animals at risk in the denominators in the calculations of
overall tumor rates in the Cochran-Armitage test to reflect less-than-whole-animal contributions
for animals that die without tumor before the end of the study (Bailer and Portier 1988). The
modification is made by defining a new number of animals at risk for each treatment group. The
*
*
number of animals at risk for the i-th treatment group R i is defined as R i = ∑ W ij where w ij
is the weight for the j-th animal in the i-th treatment group, and the sum is over all animals in the
group.
Bailer and Portier (1988) proposed the weight w ij as follows:
wij = 1 to animals dying with the tumor, and
wij = ( tij / tsacr )3 to animals dying without the tumor,
where tij is the time of death of the j-th animal in the i-th treatment group, and tsacr is the
planned (or intended) time of terminal sacrifice. The above formulas imply that animals living up
to the end of the planned terminal sacrifice date without developing any tumor will also be
assigned wij =1 since tij = tsacr. Also animals developed the tumor type being tested before the
end of the study will be assigned as wij = 1.
Certain treatment groups of a study or the entire study may be terminated earlier than the planned
(or intended) time of terminal sacrifice due to excessive mortalities. However, based on the
principle of the Intention-to-treat (ITT) analysis in randomized trials, the tsacr should not be
affected by the unplanned early terminations. The tsacr should always be equal to the planned (or
intended) time of terminal sacrifice. For those animals that were sacrificed later than tsacr,

Reference ID: 5015782

NDA 216951 (GSK1278863A)

Page 9

regardless their actual terminal sacrifice time, tsacr was used as their time of terminal sacrifice in
the analysis.
One critical point for Poly-k test is the choice of the appropriate value of k, which depends on the
tumor incidence pattern with the increased dose. For long term 104 week standard rat and mouse
studies, a value of k=3 is suggested in the literature. Hence, this reviewer used k=3 for the analysis
of this data.
Multiple testing adjustment:
For the adjustment of multiple testing, this reviewer used the methodologies suggested in the
FDA guidance for statistical design and analysis of carcinogenicity studies (2001). For dose
response relationship tests, the guidance suggests the use of test levels of =0.005 for common
tumors and =0.025 for rare tumors for a submission with two species where both are two-years
studies, in order to keep the false-positive rate at the nominal level of approximately 10%. For
multiple pairwise comparisons of treated group with control, the guidance suggests the use of
test levels of =0.01 for common tumors and =0.05 for rare tumors, in order to keep the falsepositive rate at the nominal level of approximately 10% for both submissions with two or one
species.
A rare tumor is defined as one in which the published spontaneous tumor rate is less than 1%.
However, if the background information for the common or rare tumor is not available, the number
of animals bearing tumors in the vehicle control group in the present study was used to determine
the common or rare tumor status in the review report.
Reviewer’s findings:
The tumor rates and the p-values of the tested tumor types are listed in Tables 2A and 2B in the
appendix for male and female rats, respectively. The tumor types with p-values less than or equal
to 0.05 for dose response relationship and/or pairwise comparisons of treated groups and vehicle
control are reported in Table 4.
Based on the criteria of adjustment for multiple testing discussed above, for male rats, a
statistically significant dose response relationship was noted in the incidence of fibroadenoma in
mammary gland (p-value = 0.0005), with a corresponding statistically significant increase in the
high dose group (Group 5) when compared to the vehicle control group (p-value = 0.0044),
regardless of the tumor classification (rare or common). Statistically significant dose response
relationships were also noted in the incidence of hemangiosarcoma in spleen, and adenoma in
stomach (p-value = 0.0224 and 0.0224, respectively) without corresponding statistically
significant pairwise comparisons if these tumors were considered to be rare tumors. No
statistically significant findings were noted in tumor data for female rats.

3. Mouse Study
Two separate experiments, one in male mice and one in female mice were conducted. As
indicated in Table 5, in the experiment there were four treated groups, and one vehicle control
group. Three hundred Crl:CD1 (ICR) mice of each sex, respectively, were assigned randomly in
size of 60 mice per group. Three of the four treated groups received once daily oral gavage at
0.2, 0.8, or 3 mg/kg/day of GSK1278863 for a minimum of 104 weeks for both male and female
mice. These three treated groups were also dosed via once daily subcutaneous injection of a
cocktail formulation containing the three major circulating human metabolites [2.0 mg/kg/day
GSK2391220 (M2), 2.5 mg/kg/day GSK2506104 (M3) and 1.3 mg/kg/day GSK2531401 (M13)].
In this review these three treated groups were referred to as the low (Group 2), mid (Group 4),
and high (Group 5) dose groups, respectively. The other treated group (Group 3) received 0.8
mg/kg/day GSK1278863 by once daily oral gavage + subcutaneous reference item by once daily
subcutaneous injection without metabolites. The mice in the vehicle control group (Group 1)
were administrated once daily with oral gavage at 0 mg/kg/day of GSK1278863 and
subcutaneous reference items [1% (w/v) methyl cellulose (400 cP@ 2%) / 0.2% Tween 80 (v/v) /
5 mM NaOH in Ultra Pure Water], and handled for the same duration and in the same manner as
the treated groups.
The same clinical examinations, laboratory investigations and pathology procedures used in the
rat study were performed in the mouse study.

3.1.
3.1.1.

Test Material

Sponsor's analyses

Survival analysis

In the sponsor’s report, each animal survival time was measured by the number of study days
until its death. The animal was classified as uncensored when death occurred within the
experimental period and was not accidental. Otherwise, that is, when death was accidental, as it
is the case for Animal No. 2532, and/or whenever death occurred during the terminal sacrifice
period, the animal was classified as censored. The first day of the terminal sacrifice period, Day
729, was considered as the censoring time of all terminal animals.
For each sex, Kaplan-Meier product-limit method, applied on daily intervals, was used to
estimate each group survival function for the dosing period, and to generate the corresponding
survival curves. At the 5% level, the significance of an overall dose-related trend in mortality
across the reference item group (Group 1) and the three test item treated groups having the same
subcutaneous dose level (Groups 2, 4, and 5) was evaluated for each sex via Peto’s two-sided test
(Peto et al., 1980), using the corresponding oral dose level scores 0, 0.2, 0.8, and 3 mg/kg/day. In
addition, Peto’s two-tailed test was conducted, at the 5% significance level, to compare the
mortality rates in the reference item group and in each of Groups 2, 4, and 5.
Sponsor’s findings:
The sponsor’s analysis showed that the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 34 (57%), 33 (55%), 28 (47%), 33 (55%), and 32 (53%) in the vehicle control group, the
low, mid, and high dose groups, and Group 3 for male mice, respectively, and 29 (48%), 27
(45%), 36 (60%), 26 (43%), and 32 (53%) for female mice respectively. No statistically
significant findings in mortality were noted for both male and female mice.
3.1.2.

Tumor data analysis

Regarding the tumor data, the statistical evaluation was done for each sex separately and was
limited to all non-secondary neoplastic lesions found in study plan-required tissues/sites as well
as to the combined hemangiosarcomas across whole body. Tumors listed under subcutis and
hemolymphoreticular tissue were statistically analyzed using all study animals.

Reference ID: 5015782

NDA 216951 (GSK1278863A)

Page 12

Neoplastic lesions designated as palpable and listed under mammary glands, skin and subcutis
were statistically analyzed according to Peto’s onset rate method for tumors observed in a
“mortality independent” context (Peto et al., 1980), using all animals. The onset time of each
palpable tumor was given by the first date of detection. The death time was used as the onset
time for a lesion categorized as palpable and not detected invivo. The death time of all animals
dying after the end of the experimental period will be considered to be the first day following the
experimental period. For combinations of both palpable and non-palpable tumors, the animal
death time was used in the analysis which was conducted according to the “mortality dependent”
method described hereafter for non palpable tumors. Tumors categorized as non-palpable were
analyzed using the “mortality dependent” method. In this context, each neoplastic finding was
classified as the cause of death (designated by the study pathologist as definitely or probably
fatal) or not the cause of death (designated by the study pathologist as definitely or probably
incidental or undetermined). However, all non-palpable neoplasms found in an animal were
automatically classified as incidental if the animal in question died after the experimental period.
For combinations of neoplastic findings, if an animal had one of the related tumors classified as
fatal, then the neoplastic finding defined by this combination was classified as fatal. Otherwise, it
was classified as incidental.
Neoplastic findings classified as fatal and incidental were processed using the death rate method
and the prevalence method, respectively. As per FDA’s draft guidance (FDA’s draft Guidance
for industry, 2001), the processing of incidental tumors was done by creating a single separate
interval for the scheduled terminal sacrifice period and by dividing the experimental period into
the following fixed intervals: Days 0-364, Days 365-546, Days 547-644, and Days 645-728.
Using the derived outcomes from the processing of both fatal and incidental tumors, a test
statistic was built to perform a global survival-adjusted trend test on tumor data observed in a
“mortality dependent” context (Peto et al., 1980). For each dataset of interest within each sex, the
significance of a linear dose-related increase in tumor occurrence rates across Groups 1, 2, 4, and
5 was evaluated using Peto's survival-adjusted one-tailed trend test. For both sexes, the related
oral dose level scores multiplied by 10 were used to perform the overall trend test. In addition to
the overall trend test, pairwise group comparisons were made using Peto’s one-sided test to
determine if the tumor rate in each of Groups 2, 4, and 5 were significantly higher than the one in
Group 1.
As mentioned by Lin (Lin, 1997), the discrete permutation distribution was used to compute the
corresponding p-value for each statistical test performed on a dataset containing 10 or less tumor
occurrences. The interpretation of the results should be accomplished with the consideration of
both historical control data and biological relevance. In fact, the evaluation of the carcinogenic
potential of the test item should be undertaken in the light of an overall scientific judgment.
Multiple testing adjustment:
According to the recommendations of Lin and Rahman (Lin and Rahman, 1998), the doserelated increase in tumor incidence should be considered significant if the p-value is less than or
equal to 0.025 for rare tumors and less than or equal to 0.005 for common tumors (historical
incidence of more than 1%); the increased tumor rate in a test item treated group when compared

Reference ID: 5015782

NDA 216951 (GSK1278863A)

Page 13

with the reference item group is considered to be significant when the p-value is less than or
equal to 0.05 for a rare tumor, or is less than or equal to 0.01 for a common tumor.
Sponsor’s findings:
In the sponsor’s report, based on the adjustment criteria for multiple testing described in the
sponsor’s report, no statistically significant overall trend was noted in tumor data for both male
and female mice. The pairwise comparison results revealed significance (p-value≤0.05) for the
datasets listed in Table 6Table 3.
Table 6: Significant Findings (p-value ≤ 0.05) of the Pairwise Comparison Results

Based on the adjustment criteria for multiple testing described in the sponsor’s report, Group 3
tumor incidence rates corresponding to hepatocellular carcinoma in male mice and to harderian
gland adenoma in female mice will be considered to be significantly higher than the
corresponding incidence rate in Group 1 only if these tumors are classified as rare.

4. Summary
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in rats
and one in mice. These studies were to determine the carcinogenic potential of GSK1278863A, a
HIF-prolyl hydroxylase inhibitor, when given once daily by oral gavage for up to 104
consecutive weeks to Crl:CD (SD) rats, and once daily by oral gavage and subcutaneous
injection of a cocktail formulation containing the three major circulating human metabolites for
up to 104 consecutive weeks to Crl:CD1 (ICR) mice, respectively.
Rat Study:
Two separate experiments, one in male rats and one in female rats were conducted. In each of
these two experiments there were four treated groups, and one vehicle control group. Three
hundred and fifty Crl:CD (SD) rats of each sex were assigned randomly in size of 70 rats per
group. The dose levels for the four treated groups were 0.02, 0.2, 0.8, and 4 mg/kg/day for male
rats, and 0.02, 0.2, 0.8, and 7 mg/kg/day for female rats, respectively.
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 20 (29%), 17 (24%), 17 (24%), 16 (23%), and 15 (21%) in the vehicle control, the low, mid,
mid-high, and high dose groups for male rats, respectively, and 20 (29%), 21 (30%), 20 (29%),
27 (39%), and 30 (43%) for female rats respectively. A statistically significant increasing trend
in mortality was noted for male rats (p-value = 0.0040), with a corresponding statistically
significant increase in the high dose group when compared to the vehicle control group (p-values
= 0.0143). No other statistically significant dose response relationship and pairwise comparisons

Reference ID: 5015782

NDA 216951 (GSK1278863A)

Page 15

in mortality was noted for male and female rats.
In the reviewer’s analysis, for male rats, a statistically significant dose response relationship was
noted in the incidence of fibroadenoma in mammary gland (p-value = 0.0005), with a
corresponding statistically significant increase in the high dose group (Group 5) when compared
to the vehicle control group (p-value = 0.0044), regardless of the tumor classification (rare or
common). Statistically significant dose response relationships were also noted in the incidence of
hemangiosarcoma in spleen, and adenoma in stomach (p-value = 0.0224 and 0.0224,
respectively) without corresponding statistically significant pairwise comparisons if these tumors
were considered to be rare tumors. No statistically significant findings were noted in tumor data
for female rats.
Mouse Study:
Two separate experiments, one in male mice and one in female mice were conducted. In each of
the experiment there were four treated groups, and one vehicle control group. Three hundred
Crl:CD1 (ICR) mice of each sex, respectively, were assigned randomly in size of 60 mice per
group. Three of the four treated groups received once daily oral gavage at 0.2, 0.8, or 3
mg/kg/day of GSK1278863 for a minimum of 104 weeks for both male and female mice. These
three treated groups were also dosed via once daily subcutaneous injection of a cocktail
formulation containing the three major circulating human metabolites [2.0 mg/kg/day
GSK2391220 (M2), 2.5 mg/kg/day GSK2506104 (M3) and 1.3 mg/kg/day GSK2531401 (M13)].
The other treated group (Group 3) received 0.8 mg/kg/day GSK1278863 by once daily oral
gavage + subcutaneous reference item by once daily subcutaneous injection without metabolites.
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 34 (57%), 33 (55%), 28 (47%), 33 (55%), and 32 (53%) in the vehicle control group, the
low, mid, and high dose groups, and Group 3 for male mice, respectively, and 29 (48%), 27
(45%), 36 (60%), 26 (43%), and 32 (53%) for female mice respectively. No statistically
significant findings in mortality were noted for both male and female mice.
In the reviewer’s analysis, no statistically significant finding was noted in the reviewer’s analysis
for both male and female mice.
